Itettas to the (Oita

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

THE EDITOR of " The Examiner" invitee corre-

-- spondence on all subjects of public interest. Lensus should be brief and pointed. Their object must be to impart or seek information. not abuse.

Ls-r-Tias---1;n-ii-st in all cases be accompanied by the

_ name and address of the writer. - COMMUNICATIONS received early in the week— except urgent matters—can always be gird the best attention.

SUMMER SCHOOL OF CHURCH MUSIC.

Sir,—The Summer School of Church Music made a most successful beginning last year at Oxford, and I ask the hospitality of your columns to make known the arrangements con- templated this year at Cambridge. Sidney Sus-. sex College has been lent to the " School" from August 17th to 22nd, and here lectures will be given by men of eminence in their art, the prin- ciples advocated in these lectures being illus- trated by , daily choral services in the College Chapel. Of those who join, about 40 will be given rooms in College; the rest will have lodg- ings found for them. All will have their meals together in the College Hall. The cost will be about -„e2 a head for, the week. AppliCation should be made to the Rev. A. S. Duncan Jones, Blofield Rectory, Norwich. I should add that both Plainsong and Anglican music will be dealt with.

L. J. T. DARWALL.

Walton Vicarage.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's Associa.

N.A. CENTRE.

Taking angling all round last week-end, fair sport was had. Specimens were weighed in both from the Gowy and Norton. Although they are of no surprising weight, still they are there to be beaten. Sport at Norton was reported good. The only thing to mar the angling was the lack of weighers. The same applies to the Gowy. Roach, trout, dace and 'perch were taken. The latter are get- ting quite plentiful in the upper reaches of the Gowy, and some have been secured from five to six ounces. It is said that these have found their way down from the Shropshire Union Canal, when they have been letting off the , different lengths. Well, if that is so, anglers are in for a good time amongst perch, as this is the ideal stream for them to thrive in. We also notice that the reports from llickle Trafford state that gudgeon have become quite plentiful, and they are to be taken in the upper lengths well over the ounce daily.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
MUNICIPAL ANGLING.

I notice from Mr. Phillipps' weekly reports in the "Umpire" that he is advocating that muni- cipal authorities should take up waters for the benefit of ratepayers in the different towns. I take the liberty of using the columns of our local Press for the same purpose. Why not? There are almost two thousand anglers in Warrington, ' who would readily take advantage of any such step. Warrington is surrounded with waters,, but there are few fishable, the pollution fiend having taken the place of the clear streams that were once teeming with fish. Now there are several•good spots hround War- rington that could be made into good fisheries if our Councillors would only take the matter up, and they could rely on the secretaries of the town helping them out in the matter. Take, for instance, how the bowling of the town has made rapid strides these last few years, through the same source. If bowling has made progress,: why not angling? There are plenty of well- organised clubs in the town, but the drawback is we have not sufficient waters near enough to home to make them the success they ought to , be.

THE NORTHERN ANGLER 'RECORD IN WARRINGTON. We are now able to state that the N.A. of Warrington has surpassed all previous records in membership, even when they were supposed to be at their strongest; that was the time when that glorious fishery was doing so well. I am referring to the Trent and Mersey Canal known locally as the Acton Bridge length, After the pollution of the water the members seemed to dwindle off. Our members have topped the membership of any year, since the formation of the N.A. in Warrington, and it goes without say- ing that the success is owing to the dogged working of the officials.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING MATCH.

The opening event of season 1911 (from the centre) takes place to-morrow,. July 26th, at - Aintree, in the Leeds and Liverpool Canal. The secretary has arranged with the Cheshire Lines Committee to have two saloons put on the 9.30 a.m. train for the convenience of members. ' The secretaries will be down at headquarters to-day (Saturday), from 7 till 10 p.m., to receive entries, subscriptions, railway fares, &c. Train leaves Central Station at 9.30 a.m. and returns from Aintree at 8.30 p.m., arriving at Warrington at 9.20 p.m.

The committee has decided to put up a splen- did lot of prizes, and they are expecting a good entry, especially after the success the Bay Horse Club had at the same spot. Those who fished in the Bay Horse match say it is an ideal canal for contest fishing.

All members must be straight on the books to take part in the contest. A.S.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
WESLEYAN CONFERENCE.

Mr. Runciman and Nonconformist Education Grievances.

At the Wesleyan Methodist Conference at Leeds on Saturday, Mr. E. Lindsay Blee moved a resolution deeply deploring the giving up of any Wesleyan Metho- dist school, and impressing upon managers that, before any steps are taken to transfer or surren- der a school, they should lay the ca de before the Wesleyan Education Committee. While in 1905 they had 590 day schools, they had now only 299. The decrease in the number of their Sunday scholars since 1905, amounting to about 50,000, was, he said, due to that cause.

Sir G. J. Smith pointed out that in many cases the transfer or surrender was practically -accom- plished before the Wesleyan Committee heard of it. He appealed to superintendents of cir- cuits to see that no steps were taken without their advice.

Sir R. Perks thought Nonconformist griev- ances should be remedied at once. They had been hammering at the door of the Government long enough, and the time had come when they should take some more positive action.

Mr. Runciman, supporting the resolution, said he did so because he believed they were in the transition stage with regard to education. They certainly should act with great caution. He appealed to Conference not to blame the Govern- ment because nothing had been done to remedy Nonconformist grievances, recalling the Bill of 1906, and the attempt which he had made at 4 later stage 'to arrive at a settlement by agree- ment. It was no fault of theirs, for they had been defeated only by the strength of the Oppo- sition. The Conference xvould be ill-advised if it said those attempts were to be final. One of the most urgent duties of those who held their views in Parliament was to see that Nonconformists were treated properly, not only in the towns, but In country districts. There were 6,000 par- ishes where it was a case of an Anglican school or none, and while he did not say that every Anglican school was exercising a pernicious re- ligious influence, the exceptions were enough to make it necessary to demand education free from that religious influence. They should be very careful never to surrender a school in all cases where by so doing they would create a new single school area. If Nonconformists were pre- pared to take a strong line there were those occupying responsible positions who would sup- port them to the utmost extent of their powers. The resolution was agreed to.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
Temperance.

The Temperance Committee reported that for some years there had been a decrease in the membership of bands of hope, and attributed it partly to the increasing claims made upon the time of the children by the day schools, partly to the competition of the picture houses, and partly to the decrease in the number of Sunday scholars. On the other hand there had been an increase of 40,000 in the membership of the Young Abstainers' League, which was designed for young Mahodists' between the ages of 13 and 21.

Alleged Insobriety in Mills. A paragraph in the report stated: "Drinking customs in the weaving mills (of North Lanca- shire) are so general that very few of the girl operatives remain teetotalers." It Went on: "There is most drinking when a girl operative is to be married. Every weaver is canvassed for a subscription. Many are not more than 12 or 13 years of age, and are in at the jollification." The Rev. S. F. Collier, ex-president, declined to allow the paragraph to pass without chal- lenge. He always doubted percentages, he said, and he did not know how the league secretary, who supplied the information, arrived at his facts. Perhaps he was so' keen to find non- teetotalers that he did not find the teetotalers. He believed these customs were diminishing. To state that very few of the operatives remained teetotalers was far too sweeping a statement, and was likely to dishearten many thousands of girls who remained true •and loyal. (Hear, hear.) Anher delegate moved the deletion of the paragraph, which he described as almost a libel upon the factory workers. Mr. Richard Lindley, of Shipley, declared with some feeling that some of them had sisters among these girls, and they did not " warit any of it." (Hear, hear.) The Rev. T. E. Westerdale urged the elimina- tion of any paragraph localising the evil. Eventually references to specific trades were expunged, and a comprehensive paragraph sub- stituted protesting against drinking customs among young people in industrial life.

Sunday _Schools.

The report of the Sunday School Committee stated that the decline was in numbers, not in influence, and the schools were better organised than they ever'had been.

RESPONSIBILITIES OF CHURCH FELLOWSHIP.

The Wesleyan Conference on Monday adopted resolutions directing that during the coming year all the churches should make' a definite effort to bring home to adherents the privileges and responsibilities of church fellowship, and that an organised effort should be made through- out the entire country to win those outside the churches.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 25, 1914.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
BRUTAL SPORT.

Soolal Instinct at Its Lowest.

REV. H. OGDEN SPEAKS OUT.

The Rev. H. Ogden preached on Sunday even- ing at the Golborne-street Baptist Church upon the subject, "The Church and Brutal Sport," taking for his text Romans xiii. 1: "Let every, soul be, subject ;unto the higher powers."

• The preacher said that the day was over when the Church could regard the world as an utter- ly alien force The son must go to business, even though it had not a religious basis, and the daughter must go to school, even though the company was not exactly pious. The young people spent most of their waking moments in what might be styled an " alien" atmosphere. Most flowers must be grown in the open, even though the chimneys smoke. The gardener's ambition was not to grow less flowers, but to abate the grime and the dust. The Church in its relation to men pursued a sane and sound policy in keeping down the grime and the dust.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
Purity in Business.

All honour was due to the clean journalist who never smuts his page, and the stage-man- ager deserved respect when he keeps a clean stage and a clean programme. They were making the home struggle easier. Hence the Church cannot hold itself aloof whilst the currents of life swirl by.

Our forefathers had a very simple theory, and a very simple application. Everything save the Bible, the Church, and the class meet- ing was of the world, the flesh, and the devil. All music save hymn music was the harmony of fallen angels. In their days she was an apostate who dared to play the "Song without words". upon the Sabbath day. But our fore- fathers had the blessedness of their simplicity. They were spared the danger of what is known as our "social instinct." The pleasures of this life were only snares for silly feet. '

Church-going Pleasure-seekers. To-day, probably as never in the history of Free Churches, members of the Church and congregations attend places of amusement and resorts of pleasure. Some suggest that it is a certain proof of the arrested progress of the Church. But if the day had arrived when Chris- tians could share and join in the social pleasures of life, the day had equally arrived when that which the soul loathes should. not be the fare of the amusement board. The fact that Chris- tians partook in an increasing degree in what may be called the pleasures of the world, and that the Church could not escape souk responsi- bility towards the social instincts of the race, demanded that something should be said when those pleasures became brutal.

"The Boxing Craze." , This revival of the boxing craze revealed the social instinct at its lowest. The contest at the. Olympia was called a fight, and if the in- tentions of the principals were correct, as re- ported, it was meant to be a bloody fight. Each antagonist was there to settle the matter as quickly as possible. One had the stroke of a sledge-hammer; the other could use his fists almost as a rapier. If either remained within the rules of the ring it was legitimate sport for him to maim his opponent. A.G.G." described the affair in the language of the jour- nalist. When they had allowed for the legiti- mate licence of a journalist, the scene re- mained impressed upon one's mind with horror. "A.G.G." said that it was the ugliest scene he had witnessed in his life, and scenes are the common spectacles of journalists. He described it as "foul." The defenceless man was on his knees when his antagonist struck him a fearful blow behind the ear. The operation that followed reminded him of restoring a man rescued from the water.

" Money in It." When the national taste was filled with such pleasure the social instincts were at their lowest Dr. Clifford, in writing of the craze of boxing, said it was not so sure that even the barbarity of the Olympia would not settle this craze. There is money in it, and "where the carcase is there will the vultures be gathered together:" The yellow Press would boom it because there was money in it, and when the winner of such a contest receives £4,000 and the loser £3.000 the Church found herself up against a mighty and tragic abuse of wealth.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Fortune in An Hour."

The schoolmaster, the salt of the earth, was passing rich on .t. 200 a year. He might mould

the nation, but the nation was not prepared to give him his recompense. And yet fighters re- ceived a fortune in an hour! Perhaps the most lamentable feature of the contest was the presence of women, who had been invited by the promoter because there was "money in it," and yet the promoter was at pains to tell us that his own wife never wit- nessed a contest. He evidently was at pains to preserve his own domestic felicity, whatever disruption he caused on other hearths.

Women Spectators.

We had no admiration for the Roman matrons who watched the gladiatorial fights sealing the fate of the vanquished with thumbs turned down. There was national displeasure when the present Queen of Spain witnessed the bull-fights; it was 'said to be " un-English." What could be said then of the modern woman who, in the 'words of the "Saturday Review," attended the contest, "not to see boxing, but to see blows and blood"? It reminded us more of decadent Roman life than English life, and all lovers of the good, and the pure, and the true must be anxious that the woman's taste for such scenes may not grow.

The Clergyman M.C.

One joined with the speaker at the Wesleyan Conference in condemnation of the clergyman who was anxious to act as M.C. in a fight. Happily the suggestion of his bishop was heeded. One rejoiced that the fair name of the Anglican Church was not stained by the pres- ence of a clergyman M.C. Silence had better be observed concerning the M.C. who was present. The Church had a voice and ni,ust have a policy, concerning such brutal sport. Perhaps not every individual will be subject to the higher powers in the sense set forth by Paul, but the Church must strive—and every honest citizen will strive—to see that in the measure of their light all men must be subject to the highest power they knew.

TOWN MOTOR TRAFFIC.

A Complaint by Local Trade

Unionists.

DANGER TO HORSE DRIVERS.

Pedestrians who use the streets of Warring- ton have long complained of the danger in which they are hourly placed by the rushing, raging motor traffic. Warrington, through its geo- graphical importance, stands in a more difficult position than most towns in the country, and motors of all sorts and conditions rush into it from north, south, east and west to converge in the narrow streets and imperil the limbs of the good citizens. Residents living on the chief highways have likewise complained of dust and .noise and a vibration which Dr. Joseph, in his mayoral days, described as minor earthquakes. Pedestrians and residents have now received a fresh ally in the shape of a considerable body of trade unionists, who mean to tackle the Mayor and others in the hope that a solution of the difficult problem can be discovered.

The members of the Warrington No. 2 branch of the Railway Workers' Union raised a fresh complaint about the motor traffic at a meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council on Tuesday night.

Their secretary wrote that a large section of the railwaymen, especially those controlling horse vehicular traffic throughout the town, viewed with alarm the great extent to which motor traffic had increased in town, and the grave danger in which drivers of horse traffic were placed. They suggested the introd'uetiOn of more drastic traffic regulations on the streets and better police control.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGER EVERY MINUTE.

Mr. Chamberlain (Railway Workers) said the men who drove horses fully realised the danger which confronted them every minute. There had been an alarming increase in the number of accidents, and they thought a scheme could be found whereby these could be materially re- duced. Motor traffic went through the narrow streets at too fast a speed. Perhaps of the heavy traffic, the chief offen- ders were the motor 'buses owned by the Cor- poration. The present time limit for each jour- ney should be altered so as to give the drivers more time to do the journey, and thus avoid the necessity of rushing. Under the present time limit the drivers were unable effectively' to con- trol the 'buses.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE UNIONS TO COMBINE.

Mr. C. Dukes (secretary of the Council) sug- gested that the Railway Workers, Tramway and Vehicle Workers' anZI the Carters' Unions should combine and take action in the matter.

He was afraid that certain individuals who had the name of being quick drivers were allowed to rush through as they liked, while carefully driven motor drays were held up at awkward points, which caused them great diffi- culty in re-starting. A Delegate: No notice is taken whatever by certain motor car drivers of 'foot passengers. Mr. Baker (Carters' Union) said the only thing was to limit the motors to a certain speed when passing through the town, and the Watch Committee might have power to do that. The motor 'buses were certainly great offenders, as they were not allowed sufficient time to do their Journey. _ _ _

Messrs. Chamberlain, Shimming and Dukes were appointed to arrange for a conference be- tween the three unions, and, if necessary, arrange for a deputation to meet the Mayor.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
OLD ENGLISH FAIR.

Last Day Brings Joy to the

Promoters.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0043.txt | included=true | included =====
SUBSTANTIAL PROFITS.

It seemed as if all the community of Warring- ton had flocked to Victoria Park on Saturday to partake of the multitudinous joys of the Old English Fair. Throughout the afternoon and night an incessant stream of eager pleasure- seekers swarmed through the gates, joining witholt delay in the many amusements with a zeal which must have surpassed the fondest dreams even of the most hopeful member of the Fair Committee. It is estimated that between nine and ten o'clock nearly -15,000 people were present.

Consequently- the day's takings constituted a record, amounting almost to £750. The weather on Thursday and Friday had given cause for much uneasiness, but a finer day than Saturday could not have been desired.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROCEEDS,

Thursday ,, 224

Friday

256

Saturday Subscriptions received since £1,250

It is estimated that about ,-Q6OO will represent the balance availabhl for distribution. Con- sidering that the Committee have this year un- avoidably incurred extra expense, this result is perfectly satisfactory, and quite up to the level of previous years. At the first fair, which was held in 1910, £540 profit was made out of proceeds totalling £845 17s. 10d., and at the second fair, held two Years afterwards, the taking of the large amount of .21,380 6s. 3d. resulted in £750 being devoted to the work of the Infirmary.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
BATH FULL OF COINS.

It can be imagined that under Saturday's circumstances there was no waste of time in the cash office, and the officials to whom this branch of the work was entrusted, as well as all the others who laboured so willingly in the good cause, have placed the Committee under a debt of gratitude. Such a scene as that which was witnessed in the cash office is seldom seen publicly in the town, for the utmost efforts of an increased staff of counters could hardly deal with the-thousands of coppers which were constantly being brought in, to be placed in a portable foot tin bath as quickly as they cottld be removed from the counter to make way for additional piles. These arduous labours were continued until midnight.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING CEREMONY.

A large gathering was present to witness the opening ceremony on Saturday, which was per- formed by Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., accompanied by Miss Furneaux. Colonel Charles Lyon pre- sided, and amongst others present were : The Rector (Rev. Canon Willis), the Revs. H. V. Pigot, W. Bram:camp, and H. Ogden, Mrs. Parr, Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mrs. Rylands, Mrs. Ireland Blackburne, Mrs. Edward Gipenall, Miss Joan Greenall, Mr. and Mrs. W. P. Rylands, Mr. E. J. Hall, Mrs. and the Misses Broadbent, Mr. T. Broadbent, Mrs. Joseph Harrison, Alder- man, Mrs., and Miss Smethurst, Alderman Bennett, • Councillors Jenkins, Wilson, and Hawthorn, Captain Appleton, Messrs. J. 11-1. Smethurst, L. A. Voisey, H. Colin, G. Walker, G. H. Smith, A C. Binks, H. Rogers, and J. H. J. Hampson.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL POLITICAL OPINIONS.

The Chairinan Said that that was his first appearance in such a capacity, but it afforded him great pleasure to feel that he was taking part with people of all political opinions and religious convictions in furthering a good cause in which they could all co-operate. Of all the blessings they enjoyed in this world there could be no doubt that good health was the greatest. Its value, indeed, could never be estimated. In helping the Warrington Infirmary they were doing good to the great cause of alleviating the sufferings of their fellow creatures in a way which would not otherwise be possible. The institution had been supported for the last 100 years in a most remarkable manner, and that, in the last year or two, had been largely due to the good work done by a committee, who had succeeded- in 'raising the splendid sum of one million pennies. (Applause.) All honour should be given to those workers, but it could be seen from the last annual report of the Infirmary that, in order to maintain the institution, more and greater efforts would be necessary. He very niuch hoped that increased subscriptions would be forthcoming from all sides. The large and representative attendance that day showed that the necessities of the case were appreciated. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P., AND THE GOOD SAMARITAN.

Mr. Harold Smith, who was r.ec.eivec.l. with applause, said he was pleased to join with the people of Warrington in doing something, how- ever little, on behalf of a charity so deserving and of a cause so noble. He hoped that all the men there had brought all the wages they had not already given to their wives, and that the ladies had taken the precaution of getting their weekly wages before coming there. That cause was one that must appeal to the warna- hearted and charitable people perhaps more than any other claim that could be made upon them. Referring to the parable of the Good Samaritan, Mr. Smith said :"Let us apply that parable to the present appeal. The traveller may not have been journeying from Jerusalem to Jericho; he may be wandering through the streets of Warrington. He may he at his work or he may be on a sick bed. I ask you, what- ever the cause of 'his trouble or injury, not to follow the example of those who passed by on the other side, but to assist, as indeed you have already assisted by your presence this after- noon, in pouring oil into his wounds and doing something for the poor wayfarer who has fallen by the wayside. 'Go and do thou likewise,' remembering that every penny you spend at this fete is helping some poor man, woman or child to win back that health which you and I at the present moment, enjoy. While it is never wise to preach extravagance, surely to-day I shall be excused if I ask you to be profligately extrava- gant, to spend all that you possibly dare, with the knowledge that you are not only going to derive great happiness for yourselves, but that you are rendering help to those who sadly need it." (Applause.)

VOTES OF THANKS. In proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Smith, Mr. H. Collin (hon. secretary) said they were Particularly Pleased to see Miss Furneaux with hint. (Applause.) It was kind of both to come to assist the effort. (Applause.) Captain Appleton seconded, and said that as the sun had favoured them that day, he hoped it would shine upon the future life of Mr. Smith and Miss Furneaux. (Applause.) The vote was carried with applause, and Mr. Smith replied.

ECZEMA FROM HEAD TO FOOT.

16, Leonard-street, Warrington, Lancs., Eng.— "When my little boy was six months old he had a very bad attack of eczema which spread all over his body from head to foot. He looked such a sight, for he had to be wrapped up in bandages, as the sores used to be wet. The sores came like pimples, which used to burst and sometimes the pain was so great that it sent him into convulsions. His head was such a terrible sight that I was ashamed to take him out. "This lasted for about two years. I was told he would never get better as the eczema had gotten a complete hold on his system. I then saw the Cuticura Soap and Ointment advertised and I gave them a trial. After six dressings with the Cuticura Soap and Ointment I saw an improvement, so I persevered with them. Before I had used the second box of Cuticura Ointment with the Cuticura Soap he was completely cured and he has never had any sign of eczema since." (Signed) Alfred Glover, Jan. 21, 1914. Although Cuticura Soap and Ointment are sold by chem- ists throughout the world, a sample of each with 32-p. Skin Book will be sent post-free upon re- quest. Address post-card: F. Newbery and Sons, 27, Charterhouse-square, London, E.C.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
FOUR SPSTERS MARRIED AT ONE TIME.

-.-..r...••1•1.•

Four sisters were married at St. Barnabas' Church, Bethnal Green, on Saturday. Hun- dreds of people turned out to catch a glimpse of the brides, and the church filled so quickly that the doors had to be closed to prevent overcrowd- ing. The couples were: Elizabeth Bradley and William Thomas, lift attendant; Julia Bradley I and George Skeggs, lift attendant; Florence Bradley and Alfred Heffernon, labourer; and Jessie Bradley and Thomas Cobb, a sawyer. The sisters are orphans, and lived in a house in Norman-road, Bethnal Green, with another sister who kept house for them. They had beenj engaged for some time, and they realised that if one of them decided to leave and get married the financial loss sustained by the withdrawal might lead to the break-up of the home. Accord- ingly they agreed that the best way out of the difficulty was to go to the altar together. The other sister, it is understood, will live with one of the couples. Three of the brides were attended by two bridesmaids and the other by one. They were married in order of age, the eldest first, and left the church in the inverse order, the youngest bride with her husband first. The congregation was so keenly interested that the Vicar before beginning the ceremony said " Sit down and keep quiet." The injunction was readily obeyed, and the order maintained was such that the Vicar afterwards said that it would have been impossible twenty years ago.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
WEEKLY MOTOR NOTES.

Ten hours and a quarter to do seventy-three miles over excellent roads! This doesn't sound like 1914 motoring—does it? But the amazing thing did happen and only a fortnight ago. Why? Wholly on at;count of an ill-formed car. The car itself was an automobilac delight despite its seven odd years; a car without a knock, creak, or chatter; silent as a spectre, a ' sportsman's heart-joy for speed and hill-work. But its master! Reader, what can you think of motorist who had so little consideration not only for those whom he lured away from a com- fortable home, but also for himself, as to make no decent provision against possible tyre mis- haps and the enthusiasm of the misdirecting yokel who knoweth not his left hand from his right? For it was by both these terrors that the happiness of that day was shattered. Four miles out of Tonbridge a back tyre punc- tured. We got down and looked. Fixed rim; broad mudguard well down round the wheel; and security bolts! (one bent). The tool-box yielded two straight levers of obsolete pattern, a rag, two buttons, a chop-bone and a small jack. "I believe there's a spare tube somewhere," murmured our host (?) and a search under a seat brought the poor thing to light. A new and shiny wrapper standing bravely in the spare-bucket, suggested that there was a cover. There was. It must have been the founder of its line. We were cruel to force so aged and so ' decrepit a thing to work. An hour and a quarter later we dragged our weary bones to the only four square yards of grassy sward in sight and demanded the longest "corpse revivers" our hamper could (luckily) produce. We had done it. Without a fork lever, without a dummy valve, with broken finger nails and chipped hands, we had got that tube and cover, and those security bolts (one bent) on to the wheel. Six miles from Battle that tyre burst with great enthusiasm. Nipped tube, of course. We had lunch, while the A.A. scout biked into Battle and returned with a relief car full of beautiful new covers and tubes and an assort- ment of levers that would fit any size of tyre or any kind of car wheel that ever was. In the early evening we reached Hastings. , Ran about the'place a little, and then tried to find the road to Winchelsea and Rye. Of course, we had no Guide—our cicerone scorned guides. Oh, yes! He had just screwed his eye-glass in a bit further, and asked questions of the local police, the local nut, the local tram conductor, the local yokel generally. And did they know? Did you ever find anybody in any country town who could tell you how to get to the next coun- try town? Well, Hastings could produce to us nothing human with which to prove itself an exception to the rule. We finally got landed in a cul-de-sac of sand and rocks, with pot-holes two feet deep, called Blacklands—and then curiously enough our man at the wheel knew his hearings! He had been there before, if a savage glare and gloomy silence vouchsafed my gentle inquiry to that effect, had any meanings. I would have enjoyed the story of how he first found that delicious sylvan spot. However—we got to Rye at 7.10 p.m., and I could tell an adventurous tale of carbide lamps ' gone wrong and an attempt to put them right at Hawkhurst, but will refrain. We reached the London home we have learnt to love even more than ever, at half-past one in ' the morning, and the following day I sent my friend a copy of the Michelin Guide and full particulars of the advantages of detachable rims, together with a price for converting his existing wheels. Moral. Before you go a motoring cast your eye round to see that the following are all on board— - A spare tyre, complete set of levers, spare inner tube, two gallon tin of petrol, one gallon lubricating oil, a guide, supply of carbide and a box of matches. Further-Ibefore you start her up be quite sure that every part has been gone over and greased, that your lamps are in order, and that everything is ship-shape—For then and then only you may reasonably expect to experience the joy of motoring in its Lntensity. Yours in fair and foul weather— BIBENDUM.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, J

FARNI AND GARDEN.

HARVEST AND TILLAGE.

Eff.nt of the Rains upon Cereals and Roots. It is not uncommon for the hay and corn har- vests to overlap, but this year there is the un- wonted experience in the home and eastern counties of the cutting of corn and the sowing of turnips in progress side by side. This is accounted for mainly by the extreme earliness of the harvest, hut the cultivation of the land and the sowing of root crops have been delayed by the absence of rain. The conditions that impeded tillage hastened the ripening of the corn, and the result is the simultaneous demands of two urgent and widely different tasks upon the labour of the farm.

The recent rains, although too late to affect the bulk, have greatly improved the quality of the grain. They dispelled the fear of premature ripening, and the delay has allowed the wheat and barley to fill and develop a plump kernel, and only sunshine and drying winds are needed to ensure samples of prime quality. Growers speak with significant 'reserve of the probable yield of barley.—" The Times."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOT-AND-MOUTH DISEASE AGAIN. Another Embargo on Irish Cattle.

The Board of Agriculture received information on Saturday that an outbreak of foot-and-mouth disease was confirmed late on Friday night near Roscrea, county Tipperary, Ireland. The Irish Department have scheduled an area of approx- imately 15 miles radius round the infected farm, and the Board have decided to issue an Order prohibiting entirely the landing in Great Britain of animals brought from Ireland. Ar- rangements have been set on foot for tracing to their destination all animals recently shipped to Great Britain from the Roscrea district. Any stockowners who have in their possession animals recently landed from Ireland will be well advised (the Board say') to take steps to secure their immediate isolatiOn and to notify I the fact to the local authority concerned.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
HAY.

Overmaking.

The fodder is often dried too much; and when hay has been too much weathered—that is, over-washed with rain—the same error has been indulged in, namely, too large an acreage has been cut for the staff to work with advantage.

I can conceive no greater mistake than this, especially in seasons when the weather cannot be expected to remain favourable for many days together. In the olden days it was customary for the staff of mowers to stick at their work until the whole of the grass was down; and in hot . seasons I have known them to be hard pressed by the haymakers. Nowadays it is an easy matter to treble the acreage of grass cut, while it may be questioned whether the making process has been hastened very much. It is, therefore, no hard task to get a larger breadth of grass laid low than it is possible to work with comfort, and in such cases the hay must suffer, either from being too much dried or too much exposed to the rain. The aim should be, at the earliest possible moment, to get the hay into a smaller compass so as to reduce the area ex- posed to sudden showers, and in wet seasons cocks should be more largely made. Lastly, when stacking has been accomplished, care should be taken that the roof is either well made, or protected against the weather.— Staffordshire," in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HOT WEATHER AND THE MILK SUPPLY.

The great heat is telling on the milk supply, and in the cities it may be noticed that the shops which sold a glass of milk with readiness in June now " reserve it for regular consumers." Even the British Museum cow has given out, and the demand for a glass of milk in the re- freshment room is, as the Indian student said, " severely nulled." Farmers who keep cows are at this season greatly handicapped by the pro- digious freight which railway 'companies charge . .

for ice, and see little hope of change for the better until local production of artificial ice is brought in. Milk cooling is a question of the future, but will have to be faced.—" Standard."

THE COWS IN SUMMER. Most of the cows are at grass, receiving the food of all others that favours a large secretion, while during haying and harvest the milkers are often employed on other work, and in the rush the milking is often irregular, deferred for hours after the usual time, and then performed in a perfunctory manner by tired hands, or hurried over in order to! get home to supper. During this period the cows at pasture are sub- jected to the stings of insects, sent galloping all over the fields by the "fly," and then, when heated, they stand under the shade of trees, where there is, even in light airs and on hot days, always a cold draught, or up to their ! knees and hocks in water, or in the mixture of mud and filth which at this season of the ,ear represents the contents of the pond supplying them with drink. I am of opinion that there would be fewer complaints of " sore udders," or that form of the trouble arising from inflam- mation supervening on congestion, if newly- calved Cows, and those in plethoric condition and in full milk were kept' under cover during the hours of bright sunshine and extreme heat when the pestilent fly is on the warpath. Not much grazing goes on during these hours, nor do the cows lie down and peacefully chew the cud, for when not galloping at breakneck speed, with their tails up and terror in their hearts, over the hard-baked pasture, they stand in the shade, if there is any, industriously swinging their heads aid tails, losing meat and milk, in the vain endeavour to disturb their innect foes, or congregate in the water and contract catarrh of the udder.—" Veteran,!' in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Vegetable Marrows require liberal supplies of water. Dilute with liquid manure once a week, or give a light dressing of fertiliser once a fort- night. Cut the first fruits when they are six inches long, to encourage fruiting. Feed Onions with Nitrate of Soda, guano, and a good fertiliser occasionally. Half an hour spent each evening in keeping the garden tidy will repay for the trouble. Spent Roses, Sweet Peas, and other flowers should be cut off, other flowers will follow on. Peas and broad beans should not be allowed to ripen before gathering: More pods will be pro- duced. Cut the pods off with a pair of scissors to avoid bruising the plants by pulling. Par more pods will be gathered by using them before they are really fully grown. BUDDING ROSES.

The budding of Rose trees is a most interesting I operation, and is not difficult if the directions are closely adhered to. Baying °got the stock, the common wild Briar, whic4i is the best for the purpose, they should have time to become es- tablished in their new situation. They are usually planted the previous autumn. In the case of standards all growths on the stem must be cut off, thus allowing all the energy of the plant to be concentrated in the head, allowing not more than three shoots to grow. For bush or dwarf trees all growths may be cut down close to the ground in the spring. In budding standards the buds are inserted in growth of the current season as near to the main stem as practicable. In the case of dwarf and bush Roses the buds should be inserted in the main stem either just above or below the ground level. If the latter scoop some of the soil away to enable the budding to be done easily. A PLEA FOR SWEET-SCENTED PINKS. We paid a visit to a skeleton garden at Upping- ham last June. It had been a green hillside twelve months before, so to clothe its nakedness a large quantity of the real old-fashioned small double white Pink had been planted literally all over it. It was a warm day, which no doubt helped to bring out the perfume. The gratify- ing fragrance of that garden will remain in our memory. As far as we are aware, none of the larger and newer varieties, like Mrs. Simkina or her tliajesty. are as sweet.—" The Garden."

A YEAR'S INSURANCE.

The Working of the Act in

Cheshire.

IN GOOD ORDER.

Mr. Joseph Oswald Brandrith, chairman of the Cheshire Insurance Committee, recently gave a resume of the work of the committee dur- ing the insurance year ended July 11th. He stated that during the year there had been a spirit of closer combination of the different sections represented on the committee, and upon this oneness of purpose it appeared depends the ultimate success of the administration of the Act.

Sanatorium Benefit.

The Sanatorium Sub-com—mittee had been en- able satisfactorily to meet its obligations to the extent of its demands, and its resources, had been found to be adequate for its purposes. The continuation of the provisional arrangements put into force in the first year had been carried through without any delay-to the insured per- sons with whom the committee had been called Upon to deal, and although misinformed persons had occasionally directed criticism against this particular department, the committee had at all times bee'n able satisfactorily and successfully to refute any allegations made against them. The institutional treatment recommended where desirable had been supplied at various sanatoria with which the committee had made arrangements. At the present the committee hold beds as follows, viz.; Cranham Lodge Sana- torium, 20 beds; Liverpool Sanatorium, Kings- wood, Meathop Sanatorium, 5 beds; Sanatorium, 10 beds; East Anglian Sanatorium, , Accommodation for the more advanced cases we n ie s 5 y dbeds; bed. eSI had been found at the Macclesfield Moss Lane Sanatorium, and at tile Eryngo Sanatorium, Wallasey, at each' 'of Which places the com- mittee hold 8 beds. Surgical hospital treatment has also been tg present patients are resident in Manchester Royal Infirmary, 3; Barnes' Convalescent Home, 4; Royal Southern Hospital, Liverpool, 3. re n ft granted, tinstitutio 11 : The County Council had prepared a generous and comprehensive scheme for dealing with tuberculosis, in all its, forms for all people, whether insured or otherwise, and the system of co-ordination of work outlined in their scheme bids fair in a minimum number of years to stamp out, as far as possible, the disease. CYCLING AND MOTORING.

Petrol Consumption by Light and

liea-vy Car-s.

CYCLE-SCISSORS-TO-GRIND.

As a rule when a cycle requires re-enamelling the best plan is to forward it to the makers, since they are the only ones who are able to make a really satisfactory job of it. There are times, however, when the owner has to take . .

into account the question of cost, in which case the price charged may be prohibitive.

Enamelling can be done at-home, but one must not expect to produce a really good finish, since

the portant __process_ of -sieving must be

omitted: The effect of this process is to harden the enamel and to render the application more lasting and to give it a better surface. If it is found necessary to do this work oneself, the old enamel—with the exception of the transfer— should be rubbed off with emery paper, until the entire frame is thoroughly clean. A good

enamel can be purchased at is. a tin, and this - - - - .

should be applied thinly with a fine brush. When the first coat is quite dry any imperfec-

tions should be smoothed down with fine emery

paper and a second cePat given. To produce a good result three or. four coats should be ap- plied, the rubbing down: to take place between

each as above.

Any Scissors to Grind.. 1 came across a wonderful example of ingen- uity the other day. There are many uses to which a cycle can be put, and. I think the fol- lowing is worthy of notice,. as it means. a man earning an honest livelihood.. All praise is due to him for this ingenuity in enhancing

_ the possibilities of his business by making use of his. machine. The man I saw the other day had fixed on his cycle the necessary equipment for grinding knives,, scissors, etc. In addition to this he carried a soldering apparatus. Now it will be obvious that the condition under which he works this enables him to travel , many mires a day, and rapidly at that.. lie is often to be seen at the side of the road in the district where I saw him grinding and sharp- ening. The grindstone is fixed on the backbone of the cycle, and driven from the rear wheel by a pulley. The machine is jacked up by a stand as used with motor-cycles to keep the rear wheel off the ground when standing. By pedalling while in this position he supplies the newer to his grinding and sharpening wheels. I wish this man all the luck he deserves, and hope shortly to hear that business has been so brisk and prosperous that a motor cycle will be brought into requisition. The engine could then do the work which is perfowied now by pedalling. The Value of Good Tyres.

One of the principal reasons why cycling has such a hold on the public is the comparatively low cost of upkeep. Power costs nothing and the mechanism is so simple that anyonq can master it in a few minutes. It calls for no ex- pert knowledge to effect repairs. Many are the cyclists who can completely dismantle their machines, thoroughly clean the parts and re- build them, and they have never had any shop experience. This factor also helps to keep ex- penses at their lowest. The only real expense which is inevitable if a rider will enjoy the sport to its full is in connection with tyres. No cyclist should stint himself in the matter of good tyres. They are absolutely necessary. A tyre of mediocre value will be the cause of endless trouble while it is in use. Punctures are irritating and a waste of valuable time. Therefore endeavour ghould be made to mini- mise the possibilities as far as one is able. I heard only the other day of an instance show- ing the disadvantage of using poor tyres. The rider started off on an urgent call to estimate for a printing order a few miles from his office. His tyres were poor in quality and they gave him trouble. He arrived too late to secure the order. Good, reliable tyres could have got that order.

Fuel Consumption in Light Cars. Judging by the number of light cars that one passes on the road, the only conclusion that can be arrived at is that this type of motor is popular, and, moreover, gaining in popularity every day. This ipopularity may be taken as a sure sign of their general effici- ency, but in one respect there is considerable room for improvement. I refer to the question of petrol consumption. It may not be correct, but one usually thinks that "light" is syn- onymous with "cheap." This is not the case, however, having regard to the present cost of spirit. At one time this matter might have been disregarded, but to say it is impossible to do so. I have had a considerable amount of experience with light cars, and I have found that I cannot reckon on more than 25 miles or 18 Miles to the gallon. These figures contrast very badly with the records made by larger cars. It must be remembered, however, that speed has an important bearing on petrol con- sumption, and in this case the speed was rather less than most private users attain in ordinary running. An Explanation. In the big engine there is no doubt that'the heat losses are relatively less than in the small type, owing, of course, to the greater ration of cylinder volume to cooling area of the combus- tion chamber. For this reason one cannot ex- pect as high fuel efficiency from the small- sized car. Again the tractive effort required by a car with large diameter wheels and a long wheel base is not so great as that of a short wheel base car with small wheels. The latter is much more sensitive to road inequali- ties. Lastly, in the large car there is more running on top gear, and consequently the loss in transmission efficiency is less. Taking these three points into consideration, however, the fact still remains that a light car consumes more petrol than it should, but at the same time I must confess I do not yet understand exactly where the existing discrepancy should be sought.

Large Tyres Justified. Once again the use of large tyres has been justified. The moral of the Grand Prix is the entire justification of the large tyre. The French champions, who were up to every point of the game, who were, moreover, con- fident of winning, owe their defeat to a very large extent to the one word—tyres. The French entrants relied on 120 mm. sections, whereas the winner was equipped with 135 mm. Perhaps the Frenchmen thought they would make up in speed what they lost in tyre chang- ing, but this idea proved to be wrong. After the experience the various entrants have had there was no excuse for this lack iof fore- thought.

THE NATIONAL UNION OF LIBERAL CLUBS.

A return just completed shows that the National Union of Liberal Clubs, which owes its existence to a meeting held in Manchester in June, 1907, now consists of no fewer than 520 Liberal clubs affiliated in 31 county or district federations, and having an aggregate of nearly 100,000 members. Lancashire is particularly strong in its connection with the Union, the county's quota of affiliated clubs being fully 170. Manchester forms one of the federated dis- tricts, the secretary being Mr. R. T. Anderson, of 1), WhiSton-road, Crumpsall. The whole of the work done for the National Union is volun- tary, and its rapid advance as an institution likely to be of considerable use to the Liberal party is extremely encouraging to its promoters.

At Wheelock on Saturday night the child of boatwoman named Mrs. Hannah Bell fell into the canal. The mother jumped in fully clothed and rescued the child. She was up to the neck and had the greatest difficulty in getting out.

THOS. SYMING TON .5 CO.. Eldinburgh and London.

THE CYCLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

A Holiday in the Lake District.

CHESHIRE REST HOUSES.-17.

Mrs. Booth's, Datrenham.—Between Warring- ton and Davenham there is a great gulf fixed, the awful barrier of Northwich, two beautiful towns separated by a very ugly one. Only one town in Lancashire can give Northwich points in ugliness, and the name begins with W. But Northwich has some good points for us cyclists, and the chief one is the ease with which it can be avoided. It seems to have been made on a plan which leaves it in an island sur- rounded by good roads, which take us south- ward and back again witlunit ever touching its ugliness.

To make for Davenharn, then, which lies on a direct fine through the middle of Northwich, we can make for Acton Bridge and Weaverham, and crossing the main road at Hartford we run down over the Weaver by Hartford Bridge, and are in the centre of Davenham in less than two miles. There are several good and well-known inns, and next door to the school will be found Booth's, confectioner, baker and caterer. Not much accommodation for a big party on a wet day, but a charming little lawn for teas in the garden, where half a hundred might be well served. _ _

_ Another way to Davenham is through Barnton and over the dangerous canal bridge to Win- nington Swing Bridge. The Frandley and Com- berbach roads also meet the Barnton-road at the Swing Bridge. Then opposite the old Win- nington Park Gates a road runs to Hartford Church, and provides a second access to Daven- ham. The village is a veritable garden of flowers, which is continued along the London- road to Bostock, where the late Col. France- Tlayhurst laid out a model village and the people keep it so. Davenham Church is modern, but inside very pretty, and the-coast down to the River Dane is the cyclist's ideal: Two pipes can easily be smoked on the bridge found at the bottom, for it is in a lovely spot, one where kingfishers are often seen. A mile onwards and the fine Roman road, "King-street," is struck, the high road from Middlewich to Warrington, via Lostock Gralam and Great Budworth.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
PATTER DALE.

My dear "Constant Reader," you who aon't know what I wrote about three weeks ago, I have escaped from you. Your letters can't reach me here—they must wait till I get back. There is no post from Saturday 7 p.m. to Mon- day 4 p.m. The window I sit near looks out on a maze of flowers, the brook over the wall comes down all milky from the Glenridding lead mines on the side of Helvellyn, and the big mountain throws out these great spurs which hide Lake Ullswater from us. A mile or two and we could be in the Kirkstone Pass, that received my long notice a month or so ago, and only on Friday night claimed another victim, a girl from Penrith. So sequestered is this glorious dale that the news of the battle of Trafalgar, which occurred on October 14th, 1805, never reached Patterdale till November 10th. Four of us took the boat down the lake to Pooley Bridge on Monday, and walked the whole ten miles back along the east side of the lake. It is, without exception, the , finest tramp in England for anyone but a moun- taineer. The water is at your side almost the whole time, though under Birk Fell you have to rise 200 feet above it, only to enjoy one of the grandest mountain views in the Lake district. The whole of the Helvellyn ridge is before you, from Fairfield to Great Dodd, and the big moun- tain in the middle. Strange that I met a Ger-

A MEMORABLE CEREMONY.

A " ROYAL " RECEPTION. 1 THE DOOR UNLOCKED.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
The Band and Large Crowd Give Mrs. Incidents Which Marked the Opening J. Crosfield a Heartg Welcome, of the Hall.

SCHEME OF DECORATION.

Flags, even the much treasured Union Jack, claimed by the "loyalists" in Ulster and War- rinr„ton, yea even by the Warrington Tariff Re- formers, although the flag of a Nation and an Empire, comprising all shades of political opinion, fluttered from the Liberal Club build- ings on Thursday afternoon. History shows that Liberals have even a greater right to hoist the great'flag of the Empire "in the breeze" than have the Unionists, a creation of 26 years ago, and it was onLv right and fitting it Should have figured prominently amongst the flags, banners and decorations displayed to celebrate an epoch in the splendid history of Liberalism in Warrington.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
MEMORIAL TO A LIBERAL STALWART.

The occasion was the opening Of the Memorial Hall, presented by Mrs. John Crosfield, to per- petuate the memory of one of the stalwarts .of local Liberalism, the late Mr. John Crosfield, and surely a wife never erected a more fitting memorial to a great husband. The hall is as beautiful as the hand of man can devise. It will serve a noble purpose in that it will be used for the social advancement of the people, and it is to be hoped that ere long its ceiling will re-echo the triumphant shouts of a populace, who, tired of Conservative representa- tion, have met to celebrate again a great victory for Liberalism in the enlightened town of War- rington. •

A FREE TRADE HALL. Mr. John Crosfield was first of all a Free Trader. Were not his friends Cobden. Bright

S. E. PAR. Treasi

President and lion. .•

the Association

and other great representatives of what was known as the Manchester school? The hall now open to his memory is Free Trade in every re- spect—in fact, we might call it the " Free Trade Hall " of Warrington. Just as Manchester has its Free Trade Hall, for many parts of the world has contributed to its erection and its decora- tion, and so one of Warrington's greatest Free Traders has his name perpetuated in a monu- ment which, as Mrs. A. H. Crosfield said, is as firmly founded as the Liberal party, which will exist long as a symbol that it stands, like the Liberal party, for the welfare of the people. The scene at the opening function was bril- liant. Outside, the structure was chastely deco- rated with streamers, banners and flags. Liberal colours were much in evidence, as was only fit- 'sting upon such an occasion. Crosfieltl's Prize Band pla:yed outside in Bewsey-street, where a dense crowd was assembled.

"AULD LANG SYNE." As Mrs. John Crosfield, Mr. Arthur Crosfield and Mrs. Arthur Crosfield, in a motor car, drew

near, the crowd began to cheer, faintly at first

as those on the fringe of the, throng saw them, and then louder did the cheering grow ak they

came nearer. Crosfield's Band seemed magicaLlY th take up the chord of the people's feelings, and, as the car arrived, they buist forth with the strains of • the ever'-touching song "For auld lang syne." Many an eye of the older folk was dimmed at this impressive, yea, very true, welcome of the donor of the hall.

Once inside the older part of the building, Mrs. John CrOsfield was very cordially welcomed by many of her old friends, and Mr. and Mrs. A. 11. Crosfield were most enthusiastically received.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING FUNCTION.

It was not long before the company moved along to the opening ceremony. This was briefly but pointedly performed by Mrs. John Cros- field, and details of it will be found in the ad- joining columns. It was a short ceremony, yet it appealed to the fancy of the dense crowd without, who cheered lustily as she unlocked the door that led to the cause of greater Liberal- ism in Warrington. Through the now open,portals streamed a con- tinuous line of good Liberals, not a few Social- ists, and, "whisper it not in Oath," a fair sprinkling of Conservatiyes, all, bent upon ad- miring the beauties of the new hall for War- rington.

• FLAG UNFUFILED. Mr. C. E. Parker, chairman of the Liberal Association, led Mrs. John Crosfiehl to the baleony which abuts from thee main window of the street, and upon this she likhtlY stepped to unfurl the new flag which will float over the club-4 Union Jack. In the presence of Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, several Aldermen and Councillors, and a densely. packed hall, she lightly seized the cord, and, in what the seamen call "a trice," had run the British flag mast high. The band below in- stantly struck up the National Anthem, and to the first note of "God save the King," the crowd within and without uncovered, as become 'l7iyal citizens. Thence a movement was made to the platform, from which a torrent of interesting eloquence poured for over an hour and a half. It was a memorable function, worthy of the great War- rinOonian whose name we had met to per- Petata.te. -

===== 0002601_19140725_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
BOUQUET PRESENTED.

Prior to the formal opening of the hall by Mrs. Crosfield, Mr. C. E. PARKER addressing the large crowd which had assembled- outside the building, said: We have gathered together to- day to take part in a very interesting ceremony, one which is unique in the annals of the Liberal party in Warrington. I understand the speak- ing is to be done inside the hall, so that this part of the proceedings is only formal. How- ever, I think you can recognise from outside this hall that it is, in every way, worthy of the giver and worthy of him in whose memory it is given. (Hear, hear.) I have great pleasure in asking Mr. A. Warburton to present the key to Mrs. Crosfield with which she can open the door. (Applause.) Mr. WARBURTON expressed his pleasure in pre- senting Mrs. Croefield with the silver key in a glass case, inscribed with the monogram and the , date of the opening ceremony.

• Mrs. Caosaistn then stepped forward and opened the door, saying: I now declare this building open and have great pleasure in doing so. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST GATHERING IN THE NEW

HALL.

A Delightful Experience.

After Mrs. Ziba Armitage had presented Mrs. Crosfield with a beautiful bouquet, composed of red and white roses, an adjournment was made to the new hall, where many interesting and eulogistic speeches were made to a large and enthusiastic gathering. Mr. C. E. Parker presided, and he was supported by Mrs. John Crosfield, Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield; Miss Crosfield, Mrs. Osborne, Alderman and Mrs. Tin- nion, Mr. and Mrs. Ziba Armitage, Alderman James Evans, Alderman Pierpoint, Councillors Wilkinson, Locker, Gough and Skelland, Dr. Dickson (Marlow, Bucks), Mr. W. Bibby, and Mr. E. A. Sanders.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD WISHES FROM THOSE

UNABLE TO ATTEND.

The following letters of apology were read by Councillor Locker:—

COUNCILLOR ROBERTS'S TRIBUTE. Kingsley, Hill Cliff°, Warrington,

July 22nd, 1914. I deeplytregret that I am unable to be present at to-morrow's meeting. Had the privilege been afforded me, I should have rejoiced in the oppor- tunity of personally testifying to the strong and affectionate regard in which the memory of the late Mr. Crosfield is held by all those who were at any time associated with him in public affairs.

For 29 years I was honoured with Mr. Cros- field's friendship, and there is indelibly im- pressed upon ray memory a record of his un- swerving loyalty to his convictions, his fearlees advocacy of what he believed to be right, his unmeasurable benevolence, his utter freedom from guile, his intense love of all that was pure and of good report, and his never-failing and practical sympathy with those overtaken by illness or misfortune. (Applause.) Of Mr. Crosfield's long and valuable services to educational and cognate matters, others will doubtless speak. To those of us who still feel the call of public service and to the younger men amongst us who are prepared to fight under the banner -i3l poli- tical, religious and social freedom, Mr. Cros- field's life will irresistibly appeal. I am, yours faithfully, H. ISOBERTS. —(Cheers).

'ALDERMAN BURTON'S "GOOD WISHES. 19, Stafford-road, Warrington, _ July 23rd, 1914.

I am sorry I shall not be able to be present with you this afternoon, but am more than Pleased that steps have been taken to perpetuate the memory of one of Wrrington's noblest citizens—a man faithful andlrue to his higher self. (Applause.) We all know that his dearest object was the uplifting of the struggling, labouring classes, that his hand and heart were always open to those in need; and we revere his memory.

We are exceedingly fortunate in the fact that his worthy helpmeet is still alive, carrying on his noble work. I sincerely hope that -Mrs. Crosfield will long be spared in the enjoyment of good health. She has well earned, and will always hold a warm place.in,.our hearts.

_ Yours faithfully, THOS. BURTON.

—(Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
RALLYING PLACE FOR PROGRESSIVE THOUGHT. Penketh 'Lodge, near Warrington; July 20.

Dear Mr. Locker,—lt is a matter of sincere regret that I cannot be present at the opving of the Memorial Hall on Thursday, as I shall be away from home and have been quite unable to arrange to be back in time. I am sure such a generous gift will be a great help to Liberalism in Warrington, and trust it will prove a rallying place for Progressive thought and action. Hop- ing you may have a very successful gathering, Believe me, yours very truly,

HELEN PARKER.

—(Applause.)

Dunelm, Great Sankey, near Warrington, July 22nd, 1914.

I regret that owing to unforeseen circumstances I shall be unable to accept the invitation for the opening ceremony of the John Crosfield Memorial Hall to-morrow.

I trust that you will have a successful gather- ing, and beg to thank Mrs. Crosfield on behalf of the Latchford Ward Committee and myself for her magnificent gift to the Liberals of War- rington. Yours faithfully, JOHN DODDS.

37, Upper Brook-street, Grosvenor-square, W., July 20th, 1914.

As I am leaving to-day for the Continent. I am exceedingly sorry I shall not be able to be present at the opening on 23rd inst. of the hall erected to the memory of Mr. John Crosfield. Would you please see that my regrets are con- veyed to the Chairman at the opening ceremony for my enforced absence? With regard to the piano I promised, I am advised that in view of the dampness of the new building, it would be better to defer delivery for a few months, so I have given instructions for same to be sent in October next.

Yours faithfully, HELENA SCHILIZZI.

GT, Sankey-street, Warrington, July 22nd, 1914. I aria SO sorry I shall be unable to attend the interesting ceremony to-morrow afternoon. I shall be away all day to-morrow in Virales, little jaunt I've had fixed up for some time. I hope you will have a very successful gathering in glorious weather.

I am, yours very truly. H. M. Asap. ,

DESCRIPTION OF THE BUILDINGS.

The. new buildings comprise an extension of the old club premises along the Bewsey-street frontage, increasing the latter to 78 feet. The old frontage of the club has been refaced and in- corporated in the general design of the new facade, producing a well-balanced and dignified presentation to Bewsey-street.

Looking at the buildings from Bewsey-street, one sees on one's left the new entrance and stair- case to the ground floor room and the Memorial Hall. At the extreme right of the building is the club entrance, with a retiring room in con- nection with the Hall over. The .central part of the building contains a room on the ground floor for general purposes. 32 feet long by 23 feet wide (with separate lavittory accommodation) and .the old club reading-room. At the'reat of the large room is a fully equipped kitchen (with service lift to the Memorial Hall). and a spacious porch communicating with the larg.3 room, the kitchen, the bowling green, and the old club premises.

The upper floor of the, central part of the building is wholly occupied by the Memorial

ALD. TINNION'S TRIBUTE. MRS. CROSFIELD'S 110PEi

Mrs. Crosfield's Kindness and Mr. Hall Wortho of Him to Whose MA Crosfield's Achiefemen(s. it is Erected.

"GOOD AND NOBLE WORK."

Alderman TINNION proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. John Crosfield, and expressed his pleasure at the great honour the duty conferred upon him. Mrs. Crosfield's personal charm, gracious manner and kindly, generous disposi- tion, had endeared her to all, and he thought everyone in Warrington esteemed an respected her. (Applause.) He could recall Mr. John Crosfield, and he thought it would not be out of place to state that the Liberal party had at one time a scheme for the purchase of Bank Park for a club, but Mr. George and Mr. John Crosfield were not in favour of it. Perhaps it was well it turned out so, for the people now had the benefit of the park. He wished to refer to Mr. John CroAfield's many achievements, kindnesses and generosity. In describing John Crosfield, he might be allowed to say, in the words of a famous man: "I like a man that is b. man." That was distinctly the character of John Crosfield. The Liberal Club and Association found him a liberal supporter. In 1885 the club was not in n happy position, but then Mr. John Crosfield and John Gordon McMinnies came forward and subscribed very liberally to the fund, and the club had now paid up to date.

Assisting Education.

If there was one thing they should have been proud about it was John Crosfield's assistance to education in Warrington. No one before or since had done so much for the cause of educa- tion in the town. (Applause.) ills support of the People's College would The remembered by many, as well as his help to the School of Art. They knew of his support to the Working Men's Mission at Bank Quay and Thewlis-street School, and that would be ever remembered. (Applause.) He was happy to know that in the support of the Working Men's Mission, Mrs. John Crosfield had perpetuated that good and noble work. (Ap- plause.)

John Crosfield was also a generous supporter of the Ragged School, as his (the Alderman's) friend, Councillor Skelland, could tell. In fact, there as hardly an institution in Warrington that John Crosfield had not supported in every possible way. The Infirmary had founii in him a good friend. (Applause.) Mrs. Crosfield, concluded the Alderman, on behalf of the several sections of the Liberal party, I tender to you our heartfelt thanks ,for your very generous and noble gift, and I feel sure these rooms will be used to the very best advantage for the political and social welfare of the people. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR WILKINSON SECONDS.

Councillor WILKINSON said he had much pleasure in seconding the vote of thanks. The Liberal Association would have the greatest pleasure in holding that hall to perpetuate the name of one of the noblest sons Warrington had ever produced. (Loud applause.)

The resolution was most heartily earrien, the dense audience rising and cheering enthusiastic- ally.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN LIBERALS' LOVE FOR MRS. CROSFIELD.

Mrs. Osborne Voices Their Appre-

dation.

Mrs. Osborne, on behalf of the Women's Liberal Association, presented Mrs. Crosfield with a handsome silver inkstand, inscribed as follows: _

Presented, on the occasion, of her opening the John Crosfield Memorial Hall, to Mrs. J. Crosfield by the Committee and Members of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association as an expression of their affection and their apprecia- tion of the constant and invaluable services she has rendered to the town of Warrington." Mrs. OSBORNE : I have received the following letter from Miss Bessie Dutton:—

Moore,

July 2?nd, 1914.

It is a great disappointment to me that another and previous engagement prevents me being present to-morrow at the opening of the T.C. Memorial Hall, so generously presented by Mrs. Crosfield to the Liberal townsmen, to whom she and her family have always. been such constant benefactors. Beloved and bon- mired, not only by the Liberal party, but by all, Mrs. Crosfield will ever be. Ald I feel it a deprivation to have to miss being present on this notable day, when she will kindly open the handsome premises her liberality has provided.— With regrets, believe me, most truly yours, BESSIE DUTTON. —(Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Growth of Women's Association. "

Continuing, Mrs. Osborne said: The members of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association have asked me to associate them with the thanks to Mrs. Crosfield for her very handsome gift. It is 23 years since the Association was formed, and during that lengthy period we have been privileged to hold most of our meet- ings in ,the rooms of the Club. In the early days the accommodation was quite sufficient for our purposes, as I remember at the first annual tea meeting I attended there were 67 present, and that was considered an excellent gathering. The Women's 'Liberal Association, however, has grown so rapidly that for some years past we have been compelled to engage rooms in different ' parts of the town. Instead of 67 members, we have averaged 300 in each ward. (Applause.) We have a sewing class with an average weekly attendance of 100, and we have been compelled to refuse to enrol others on account of the lack of accommodation. (Hear, hear.) I mention those facts to show you what a great boon the Memorial Hall will be to us, and you can readily understand how much we appreciate the thoughtfulness and kind consideration of Mrs. Crosfield in making provision for our members as well as those of the men's associations. We shall have ideal rooms for our work, and it would be impossible for me to say how much'we owe to Mrs., Crosfield. (Hear, hear.) We have attempted to show our thanks in the small souvenir which I have been deputed to ask you (Mrs. Crosfield) to accept. It is a gift sub- scribed for readily, cheerfully, and most will- ingly. In fact, I have never before known a request for donations to meet with such a hearty response. It is truly a gift from the heart of every member, and in accepting it to commemo- rate the opening of the John Crosfield Memorial Hall you will give unbounded pleasure to every woman associated with our cause.

I ask you to accept it as a token of our great love for you. (Applause.).

===== 0002601_19140725_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====

soap works at Bankey, which are very com- plete, but the trade is a losing one." (Laughter.) Apparently my grandfather had to face bad times in trade, for the first two years of his enterprise, because in December, 1816, my great-grandfather writes again in his diary : " Joseph came from Warrington. He stayed with us a few days; he appears in good spirits, notwithstanding his trade has been a very losing one." But the next year things evidently took a turn for the better, as my great-grandfather writes on September 28, 1817: "I attended meetings (Quarterly Quakers' meetings) at Warrington, number much re- duced to what it was forty years ago, when I became a member thereof. Son Joseph appears in good spirits, his trade being much better of late."

An Old-Time Love Story.. Then I read an entry made on the 15th Jan- uary, 1818 : " Returned from Preston with ray A.C. and son Joseph in a chaise. Joseph went on horse- back across the sands to visit his love at •Bay- cliffe; he appears in high spirits, his business has been profitable for about a year, by which he has regained his previous heavy loss." And again on June 9 of the same year there is this entry : " Son Joseph went over the sands to visit the object of his love, Elizabeth Goad." While three months later, on September sth, we learn that—

"In the evening our son Joseph again made his appearance, and the following day crossed :the sands on a visit of love to the object of his affection at Baycliffe." What I have just read will no doubt prepare you for what follows, for an entry the next year informs us that—

1819. "Our sons John and Joseph arrived; the following day proceeded across the sands on a matter of some importance, Joseph proposing to lay before Swarthmore monthly meeting his intention of marriage with Elisabeth Goad."

_ And on April 28 of the same year the marriage was celebrated.

Mr. John Crosfield Born.

And so ray grandfather and grandmother came to make their home at "Mersey Bank," and there years afterwards my father was born, and in the early years of boyhood would have con- stantly before him a striking example of the simple and strenuous life, because on the one hand all the old Quaker traditions of simplicity were maintained in the family, while at the same time my grandfather was a prodigious worker. Besides the Soapery at Bank Quay, he was concerned in half a dozen other businesses, and yet, as I have heard my father say, often enough my grandfather'Would devote the whole of his day up to five o'clock of an afternoon to public affairs, then devote the next five hotirs fo his own business correspondence, and at ten o'clock in the evening sit down to read, and read' till two in the morning. He was a great reader and a great admirer of Milton and Byron, and on Sunday mornings, when he would often drive the family over to the old Mission House at Framley, if the Spirit did not move some of the brethren or sisters there to hold forth in dis- course, my grandfather had a habit of slipping "Paradise Lost" into his hat and reading it there. Although, with the over-strenuous life he led, my grandfather only too literally and unfortunately, "burnt the candle at both ends," he was ever ready-to find time to give counsel and advice to numerous friends of his, as is re- corded in the .inemorial of him written at his death in 1844: At nine years of age my father went to a Quaker school at York, and certainly the fibre and the British sturdiness of the lads there was not weakened by any enervating luxury. The plains of York can be cold enough in the winter, and with only one black stove to heat a large schoolroom, the boys would pull off their shoes and stockings and run round the playground in the snow barefooted to warm themselves. Holidays they only had once a year. Butter was a luxury ,unknown to them, and therefore, as you may imagine, the advent of a hamper from home was a very great event. It is recorded that on one occasion, when my grand- mother sent my father a hamper containing, amongst other things, nine mince pies, he ate those nine mince pies straight off on its arrival. They had not the slightest ill-effects—but then my father had one of the finest constitutions with which a Ilan was ever blessed; and some of my earlier recollections of the' life we led lati Lymm recall to me my father hurrying througr his breakfast in less than ten minutes and rush- ing off to catch the train to Warrington, while I proudly tried to keep pace, running full tilt alongside of him to the station, my father cheer- ing one on to keep up with him, saying one would be the , fastest runner in Lancashire. (Laughter.) Keen on work, he was always keen on athletics, too, and some day, perhaps, one may have a chance to refer-to other incidents in his middle age and later years. But my time is up now, and I thought this afternoon, perhaps,l some of you might care to hear these few inci- dents of his early years, and these few remarks in reference to predecessors in the family. (Loud applause.) I again thank you for giving me this opportunity to read' these quaint ex- tracts from my , great-grandfather's diary. (Applause.) •
