PROGRESSIVE LABOUR LEAGUE.

Mr. E. Wynne Jones and Great Warrington Names.

Mr. E. WYNNE JONES). President of the Pro- gressive Labour League, expressed the thanks to Mrs. Crosfield on behalf of the League of which he had the honour to be president, and said he had read a speech delivered by Mr. John Crosfield during the strenuous times in Warrington, 1895-96, in which he said: "There is room in the Liberal party for all shades of progressive opinion," and he was very glad to find that there would be a home there for all shades of progressive opinion in Warrington. It would be of lasting use and benefit to them, for they had a beautiful hall in which to discuss the many great questions which affected them. They wanted to perpetuate the Inemory of a good, able, and enthusiastic man, and no more fitting memorial could be arranged than that one. (Hear, hear.) Mr. John Crosfield had said there were some things which made his blood boil, among them being injustice and want of fair play. There was a great deal of that in the world to-day, and whilst that building would ever stand as a memorial to Mr. Crosfield, the best way to show their apprecia- tion, love, and respect, and regard for the work Mr. Crosfield did in the past was to let their blood boil and carry on the work to completion. (Hear, hear) A heritage of great names was worth something, and there were some great names attached to their party in Warrington, and they could show their appreciatign and re- gard for the work those people had done in the past by carrying on the great work of educa- tion. With regard to the other gift of £lO,OOO by Mr. George Cros- field for their splendid park, he might say that no arithmetic had yet been found out' that could tabulate the great good, the pleasure and

MR. ZIBA ARMITAGE AND THE COMING FIGHT.

• ME. ZIBA ARMITAGE said he was very glad as an old Liberal to see that extension of the club and know its source; also, that it was dedicated to a very old friend of his. (Hear, hear.) There were some things about the. building which attracted his attention at once. The window with the balcony outside recalled to him the little window from which they used to announce the -great results. They all remem- bered the times of excitement when the an- nouncements were made from the balcony, and he only hoped that the window would witness something rather better than they had lately, and that it would be one of the best results they had ever seen. (Cheers.) They were highly pleased with the beauty of that ball and the convenience of the rooms connected With it, and he trusted that good use would be made of it in establishing the Liberal cause, so that they need not fear entering into any fight to which they were called. (Applause.)

MRS. JOHN CROSFIELD AND TIIE PRESENT.

Prior to Mrs. Crosfield's address, Mrs. JOHN CROSFIELD said: I thank each nember of the Women's Liberal Association most sincerely for their beautiful and. charming gift, which I appreciate more than words can express, because of the kind thoughts and affection Which prompted it. I shall find it most useful, for, as I said in thanking the Women's Liberal Sewing Party for their handsome writing-case, which they gave me on the occasion of their recent visit to Bryn Eithin, "a great tart of my time is spent in writing," in fact, as Miss Crosfield said one day when she was asked where I was, "Oh, you will probably find her, .as usual, chained to the writing-desk." , This is a gift that will not be put aside, but which will be, in constant use and help to re- mind me of this memorable and happy day. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
'IIM

MRS. ARTHUR CROSFIELD.

blues Association's Gratitude for the NIL

"AN IDEAL LIBERAL WOMAN."

Mrs. CEOSFIELD received a very hearty welcome, and said.: I am not generally diffident about , speaking, for I have plenty of words at my command, especially when I get angry. (Laughter.) I have not, however, enough words at my command to express the deep gratitude we women feel to Mrs. Crosfield for the beauti- ful! hall she has given to us.. More especially, I will try to tell her how really grateful we are for the rooms she has specially reserved for us women. (Hear, hear.) We are glad to see it is a woman who has realised and sup- plied this long-needed and long-felt want (Ap- plause.)'' I know that in some quarters there has been disappointment at the delay in the time it took for the hall to be erected. If we want a hall. to be well and truly built it must occupy time. The building as completed is very solid, and the foundations are as strong as the foundations on which Lib- eralism rests. These walls are strohg enough to carry what they will be required to carry, just as the walls of Liberalism are strong enough to carry the people's Bills to the House of Lords. These rooms are spacious and airy enough to admit all those with Liberal and Progressive ideas. (Hear, hear.) We can cheer our leaders without fearing that the whole fabric will crumble away beneath our feet, as the fabric of Tariff Reform crumbles away when any of the Tory leaders open/their mouths to speak on such a subject. (Hear, hear.) This is indeed a building of which we can be proud, and it will last through the ages, each year adding to its beauty and dignity. This is a striking example of the noble liberal feelings ' held by an ideal Liberal woman.. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
DOCTOR PRAISES THE INSURANCE

Medical lien's Opposition Has Vanished.

Dr. DUNBAR DICKSON (Mrs. John Crosfield's brother) said that was a day of happy memories, full of hope for the future. After speaking of his earlier connections with Warrington, the spqaker "said that Mr. John Crosfield was the means of endowing him with the spirit of Liberalism, which he was glad to say—although he was born in, Belfast, baptised in an Irish church, confirmed in Warrington in 1 an English church, and married in the Scotch church—(laughter)—ileaven only knew where he, would end—that he had been able to stick to it ever since. (Applause.) Mr. John Crosfield was a man who bore his character in his face, and he had no doubt that that magnificent hall in the future would be adorned by his portrait. They would read in his face that he was at heart an honest, straight- forward Englishman, one always ready and will- ing to help the oppressed and give help to the down-trodden and the suffering. <Applause.) Mr. Armitage, whose name was familiar to him, had mentioned that they had had victories and also defeats in the past. He came from the county of Bucks, where Liberal victories were few, though they were looking forward to success when plural voting had been abolished. (Ap- plause.) No reasonable, sensible man could be- long to any party but that to which they that afternoon were all proud to_belong. The Tory party offered people nothing.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
Medical Men Benefit by Insurance.

Speaking, as a doctor, on the great Insurance Act, he said' the Tories wanted to make the contributions voluntary. In Bucks, he might say, that whereas 95 per cent. of his fellow mem- bers of the medical profession were opposed to that Act at first, it was a great financial benefit to every man jack of them. He believed there was not one man who went on the panel who wished for the Act to be made voluntary. (Ap- plause.) He acted for his profession on the ' County Council Insurance Committee, and he came in touch with the friendly society mem- bers. He also believed that there was not a friendly society member in the country who wanted the Act to be worked on a voluntary system. (Applause.) They had worked amicably together, and the result had been that harmony had reigned amongst them, and that they in Bucks had saved enough money to treat not only the insured persons themselves, but their w*es, children and dependents.

After referring to the other great measures which the Liberal Government had passed, the doctor said he would like to thank them for the kind reception they had given to his dear sister. She and he had had many happy times Together, and they also knew what sorrow was, although it had helped to knit them closer together. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
An Old Workman's Praise.

He would like to read part of a letter Mrs. John Crosfield had received from one of the oldest workmen at Bank Quay (Mr. Nathan Hough, 93, Liverpool-road) : " I see you are coming to Warrington to open the Memorial Hall in memory of Mr. Crosfield. I was thinking this morning as I lay in bed of the many times he came along the river to the works from Walton Lea, how he used to smile, or speak a good word of cheer to me, and others, of his Workpeople. I fancied I could see him now. Nothing is too good that you can do in memory of so good a man as he was. I write this with tears in my eyes." (Applause.) He (the speaker) proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN JAMES EVANS'

PLEASURE.

Alderman JAMES EVANS seconded, and said he thought that was a red-letter day in the history of Warrington. He had never seen anything in connection with the material side of Liberalism that had pleased him better than the ceremony that day. It was a very beautiful and substan- tial building, and only fitting in perpetuation of the memory of a great man. He had hhd the pleasure of knowing Mr. Crosfield, and he had always looked upon him as one of the trusted, one of the true, as well as one of the most capable leaders of the party. In following in Mr. iCrosfield's footsteps, he always thought they were doing the right thing. They had had many great men—Mr. Crosfield, Mr. McMinnies, Mr. Rigby and others, whose names were sacred and dear to them. They all hoped the men and women would go forth from that building and carry the banner that denoted Progress, Liberty, Freedom and Equality to the nations that were as yet unborn; that the old song of liberty and freedom would be sung to the chriaieri who were to constitute the nation of the future. (Loud applause.)

PREPOSTEROUS BRAG.

The Apprehensive Ulstermen and " Galloper Smith."

NO CIVIL WAR.

The special speaker advertised to address the

weekly meeting of the Progressive Labour League on Monday did not put in an appearance at Bridge Foot, but the disappointment of the large crowd which assembled was minimised by the fact that two excellent addresses were de- livered by local speakers—Mr. E. Lunn, who pre- sided, and Mr. J. Johnson.

INJUSTICES TO SHOPKEEPERS. Landlord-made Laws Responsible.

Dealing with 'the magnificent and urgent pro- posals for the reform of the land system," Mr. Luxx said that most of the disabilities from which they suffered were due to landlord-made laws. Thg farmer had a grievance against the present landlord; the agricultural labourer had still a greater grievance; and the shopkeeper was only allowed in his shop on sufferance. A case came under his notice; not 100 miles from there, in which a man took a shop a few years ago and had built up a fine business. He was turning over £36 a week, and now his landlord had offered the shop to the man's chief competiter. They contended that such a procedure, was neither just nor right, and said that, if a man had improved the value of his premises and the landlord turned him out, the shopkeeper ought to be able to get compulsory compensation. (Hear, hear.) They were all paying more in rents, rates and taxes, as well as travelling ex- penses, than they ought to be doing if only the God-given land of the country were used for the greatest good of the greatest number: (Heir, hear.) _

" Carson's Braves."

Reverting to the present political situation, the speaker said he saw that a leader writer in "The Times" had advised the House of Lords to pass the Home .Rule Amendment Bill, because, he said, if they threw it out it would briiig civil war in Ireland appreciably nearer, and in that case the nation would say to the Unionist peers: "You had an opportunity to table terms to bring about peace and you did not use it, therefore, you are responsible." That was from a Conservative leader writer in a Con- servative newspaper. He (the speaker) read that day that news had come along that the king had called a conference between Sir Ed- ward Carson, Captain Craig, Mr. Redmond, Mr. Dillon, Mr. Asquith, Mr. Lloyd George, Mr. Bonar Law and Lord Lansdowne, that they might confer and try to find some terms upon which the question could be settled. Why was there all that anxiety to come to a settlement? A few months ago Sir Edward Carson was talk- ing about marching from Belfast to Cork with an Orange army. (Laughter.) They went about saying that the Nationalist Irishmen were an inferior race and incompetent to manage their own affairs. Where had all that talk gone, and why? In his opinion it had gone because there had come into existence a Nationalist army, comprised of 144,000 men. If Carson ever left Belfast with the idea of marching to Cork, he would not only have to meet the might of the British army, but 144,000 of the finest fighting men the army had produced. They would never do such a thing. That preposterous brag had gone and for a good reason too. They were be- ginning to feel apprehensive lest John Redmond took a leaf out of their book and invaded Ulster. Such an idea had greatly affected a number of Carson's braves. They seemed apprehensive that they .would tO' let Off a jun in real earnest.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
A Bank "f'Hold- Up."

He (Mr. Lunn) 'never believed in civil war in Ireland because, in his opinion, the banks would put the "tin hat" on any such proceeding. He was told it cost ..t6,000 per week to keep the Ulster Volunteer organisation going. Suppose they did rebel and went to war, it would mean r that they would have to put 20,000 men in the field to make any show at all. Everyone of the men would be drawn from the wage-earning classes and the banks knew it. The factories of Ulster would all have to close down—another money-making organisation would be gone, and the banks knew that also. There would notobe the slightest prospect of success. The whole idea was absurd. Where would the necessary £lOO,OOO or .MO,OOO per week come from? (A Voice: "Out of the working men's pockets.") If Carson thought the banks would advance the money he was entirely mistaken. The £1,000,000 they had got was only an indemnity fund. That money wbuld not be paid until war had broken out and someone had suffered. That was an ad- ditional reason why a spirit of conciliation was stealing over the matter. There would never be civil war in Ulster. They must not let the lurid pictures of awful havoc to follow the pass- ing of the Home Rule Bill—painted so often and persistently by Carson and his henchmen— trouble them. Galloper Smith's trousers would never be worn into holes by exertions he had to make on horseback, nor would his long face be made longer by having to take large quantities of Irish stew. (Laughter and applause.)

A Past Issue. After all, Home Rule was a past isstin. No power in England or Any other country could prevent that Bill being put on the Statute Book, and it was as well that they looked on such ques- tions dispassionately and carefully. (Applause.s Momentous Land Proposals: No Lawyer M.P.'s Wanted. _ -

The land proposals of the Liberal party were the most momentous that had ever been put • before the country ,in our time. He was sure, that, when we understood' what- it was that - .... ' i Lloyd George and the Government meant to do, . we would change the member for Warringt4n at 1 the next election. (Hear, hear.) That was what ' N they were after. They did not want a lawyer to . (Hear, hear.) For the represent them. They wanted a man who knew, , ti and understood the people and had given his . men to represent them who could look after ' their interests and work,for the general well- being of the great masses of the people. (Ap- life for plause.) i I. betterment their o f uplifting. the wholecountry they must send

DISABLEMENT BENEFITS.

The Insurance Act and a strong defence and advocacy of Free Trade formed the substance of Mr. JOHNSON'S speech, and, after eulogising the enormous benefits derived from the great Insur- ance Act, he said that the last, but by no means least, beneficent clause in that Act had come into operation—the disablement benefit. If a man had made 104 payments he was entitled to disablement benefit of ss. per week until he was 70 years of age, when he came into his old-age pension. If a man had made 52 payments the remainder could be made up, and in ten years he would have drawn £l3O. (Applause.) The Insurance Act had been criticised and depre- cated by some people, but he would ask them to sink their selfish feelings and think of the Act as being beneficial to the great majority of the people. It was going to help a great deal in making a brighter, happier and nobler England. (Applause.) Free Trade Benefits. In regard to Free Trade, the speaker said that such a policy was of the greatest benefit to working class. At the bottom of columns in " Daily Express" the following statemernt nad Re- appeared for months and months Tariff as about the form means work for all." That w uttered. ever biggest political lie that had e!' been - After a time it was altered to Tariff Reform that will work." means work for all those (Laughter.) amples x of, the failure After giving striking e n said that in re- . TIIE KING'S CONFERENCE

The Conference called by His MAJESTY to consider the possibility of effecting a settlement of the trouble raised by the attitude of the Ulster Orangemen has made an unprecedented stir in the country. Liberals and Labour men were not the only sections among our public men who saw danger in the innovation which, by implication, told us that the representatives of the people were not competent to bring a political question to a satisfactory con- clusion. Minds went back a few months, and the feeling engendered by the Curragh incident was fanned into life again, and there were suspicions that the conspiracy to defeat the Parlia- ment Act, by setting up another kind of veto, had advanced another step. Mr. ASQUITH—We shall soon have to call him ASQUITH the brave—has again stepped forward and taken full respon- sibility for the departure with all its novelties and all its risks. It must, of course, be remembered that if the latest procedure was exceptional so were the circumstances. There are two sides straining every nerve not to be com- pelled to give way to the other, and those who sought to find a means of accommodation are not to be lightly blamed if they sought the KING'S powerful aid in a constitutional way. The fire having been lighted, the flame § were fanned into a fierce con- flagration by an unfortunately-worded speech His MAJESTY delivered to the delegates to the Conference. The KING told them that "the trend has been surely and steadily towards an: appeal to force, and to-day the cry of civil war is on the lips of the most responsible and sober-minded of my people."

Many Ministerialists- asked them- selves, in all seriousness, whether "responsible and sober-minded" was not an invidious designation intended to apply to Tories only, and whether in the assumption that there is an inevit- able trend to collision, unless outside intervention takes place, and that an outbreak in North-East Ulster would be "civil war," not mere sedition, the Tory standpoint has been accepted by implication...

'the " cry " of civil war has been on the lips of Tories and in the columns of Tory newspapers only, and it was at once inferred that His MAJESTY was taking sides. Such a thing is incon- ceivable; but he has the Tories to thank for the misunderstanding. They have not only cried civil war, but they have assured us that the KING will not sign the Home Rule Bill; and it was perhaps natural that people should have seen confirmation of the Tory tale in this speech. Again Mr. ASQUITH comes forward and says the sentence was meant to include such as himself. We do not think he has been " crying " civil war. He may have referred, and in very severe terms, to those who have raised that cry, but he has not done the crying. Royal speeches should not be written in such an ambiguous manner.. .

It has become increasingly clear that the real object of Ulster Orangeism, or of Orange supporters in this country, has been to bring about the fall of the Government, but the Govern- ment are not given to falling; and this latest crisis, like the Irish officers' es- capade, has only strengthened their hands. A large meeting of Liberal members placed on record 'an em- phatic opinion that it is the duty, of the Government to complete the whole of their programme under the Parliarnent Act before a further appeal is made to the constituencies." The importance' of this representation to the Govern- ment consists in the indication which it gives that a large number of Liberals, within and out of Parliament, believe that the Opposition are not actuated so much in any concern for the Irish Protestant and Tory Minority em- bedded in a great majority Of Catholics and Na tiona 1 ists; a's by the' desire to kill the Parliament Ad.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PREFERENCE.

There was no discussion at last Monday night's meeting of the Education Committee upon the strange correspondence that has taken place between the managers of Holy Trinity School and the Education Authority respecting the staffing of Haat school, but a statement made by Canon Stevenson, when he presented the minutes of the School Management Sub-com- niittee, showed the managers to be altogether out of court. They had claimed "an efficient, experienced and well qualified teacher to assist Miss Davies at once," and as the committee had not hustled to supply that requirement they reported that "in order to safeguard the trust. deed of the school" the managers "have been compelled to give Miss Bullen notice that her services as head mistress cannot be continued and will terminate three months from this date." Canon Stevenson explained that Trinity School had been dealt with in. precisely the same manner as any school in Warrington, in similar circumstances, would be dealt with; and he, as most other reasonable people will be, Was at a loss to understand the connection between the dismissal of an infants' head teacher and the safeguarding of the trust-deed. To claim that a school is badly staffed and then cut down the staff still further because an effort to get it unfairly enlarged has failed is a most contra- dictory piece of business. However, the com- mittee resolved that they could not add to the staff because the school is already efficiently staffed in accordance with the regulations ap- plied to all elementary schools in the borough; that they did not consent to the notice of dis- missal being, given to Miss Bullen ; and that the Director be instructed to communicate this decision to Miss Bullen, with an expression of the committee's continued confidence in her. Altogether, the managers appear to have had a bad time of it in the encounter; but the days for preferential treatment, even for Church schools, are gone.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN AT CINEMAS.

The Education Committee passed a resolution on Monday night strongly urging the Watch Committee to reconsider their decision not to stipulate to the licensees of cinema houses that children under fourteen should not be allowed in those places during the hours that the ele- mentary schools in town were open. The Mayor, speaking as chairman of the Watch Committee, seemed to hint that they had been influenced to a great extent by the fact that the cinema houses had been singled out for this particular treatment, but when Mr. Kingdon pressed that point and said the Medical Inspec- tion Committee would be glad to include all places of entertainment, if the Watch Com- mittee would agree, his Worship said he could not pledge the Watch Committee.

EVERYWHERE.

The less a man thinks about his sincerity the more he is likely to have.

Some churches seem to mistake the dinner bell for the meal.

People who live in a bread and butter world are always hungry.

It's funny how long it takes arithmetic to get a child when the measles can grab him in a minute.

The Lord loveth a. cheerful giver and a man who raises thunder when things are being taken away from him.

A Truthful Pedlar. Mrs. Byers: All the big berries are on top of this box, I suppose? Peddler: No, mum; some of 'em are on the top of the other boxes.

Too Learned. A schoolboy recently wrote:—"Much butter is imported from Denmark, because Danish cows have greater enterprise and superior tech- nical education to ours."

The Cause. Bacon: "I understand some of your hens have stopped laying?" Egbert: "Two of them have." Bacon: "What's the cause?" Eghert: "A motor."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
Powerful Mushrooms.

Some idea of the extraordinary driving force imprisoned in succulent young mushrooms may be gathered from the fact that through a shop floor laid down in asphalt 3in. in depth, in Dunedin, New Zealand, several mushrooms, from two to three inches in diameter, have forced themselves up into the light of day. The asphalt looked very much as if a pick had been at work, so damaged was it by the mushrooms.

Upon the Putt ' The best pun in the English language is Tom Hood's:— " He went and told the sexton,

And the sexton tolled the bell." The worst pun is that of the man who fell into the ditch and rose with the remark: " How very ditch-stressing!" Dr. Johnson said that the pun was the lowest order of wit, but to this 'Goldsmith replied: ' "The pun, in other words, is the foundation of wit, eh?" ' Every Latin master likes to tell his boys two puns. The first is a punning derivation of restaurant. " Res, a thing; taurus, a bull; a bully thing." The iother is a derivation of virgin: "Vir, a man; gin, a trap; a man trap." Among newspaper humorists the pun is dying out. In the old days the good old days, the Burlington Hawkeye man and the Norristown Herald man and the Arkansaw Traveler man would pun at each other like this: "We don't care a straw what Shakespeare said—a rose by any other name would not smell as wheat.—Arkansaw Traveler." "We have made an oat of the above.—Bur- lington Hawkeye." "Such puns are barely tolerable; they amaze us, they arouse our righteous corn, and they turn the public taste a-rye. —Norristown Herald."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 25, 1914.

•

'

===== 0002601_19140725_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
KING CALLS CONFERENCE.

To Discuss Outstanding Irish Differences.

A VARIED RECEPTION.

\ .

The Prime Minister, rising immediately after question time in the Aouse of Commons, on Monday, announced that the King had sum- moned a Conference of party leaders to discuss the Ulster situation at Buckingham Palace. The following party leaders were named as members of the Conference, Mr. Speaker pre- siding : Two members of the Government: Mr. As- quith, Mr. Lloyd George. Two leaders of the Opposition: Mr. Bonar Law, Lord Lansdowne.

Two leaders of the Nationalist party: Mr. John Redmond, Mr. Dillon. Two leaders of the Ulster Unionist party: Sir E. Carson, Captain Craig. Apart from the position it will take in history as a departure from usage—though not consti- tutional correctitude—the conference will be memorable as that on which, for the first time, the Monarch received the Irish party leader in his capacity of a party leader.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
TO MEET A GRAVE SITUATION.

Mr. Asquith said: With -th-e-i'nd-u-lge.n-ce-of the House, before we proceed to the orders of the day, I should like to be permitted to make a very brief statement. I am authorised by the King to announce to the House that, in view of the grave situation that has arisen, he has thought it right to summon the representatives of the parties, both British and Irish, to a Conference at Buckingham Palace, with the object of discussing outstanding differences in relation to the problem of Irish government. That invitation was addressed to and has been accepted by two representatives of the Oppvition, two representatives of the Ulster Unionist party, two representatives ,of the Irish Nationalist party, and two representa- tives of the Government; and I am glad to add that, at the King's suggestion, Mr. Speaker has consented to preside over the Conference— (general cheers)—which I hope will begin its proceedings to-morrow. We shall not proceed to-day with the second reading of the Govern- ment of Ireland (Amendment) Bill, but shall ask the House to proceed with other orders.

MR. BONAR LAW'S ACCEPTANCE. Mr. Bonar Law: After that announcement I think at this stage it is neither necessary nor desirable that I should say more than that we have loyally accepted the invitation—the com- mand—of his Majesty to attend a Conference to-morrow. (Opposition cheers.)

NATIONALIST ATTITUDE. Mr. John Redmond: Perhaps I may be allowed to say one word? My colleagues and I have no responsibility for the policy of calling this Convention. (Ministerial and Nationalist cheers.) I do not think I am called upon to express any opinion as to whether, in the result, it will prove useful or the contrary. The invi- tation to attend the Conference came to my hon. friend, Mr. Dillon.. and myself, in the form of a command from the Bing, and we, of course, accepted it.

BREACH OF CONFIDENCE. Sin Henry Dalziel (L., Kirkcaldy Burghs) asked the Prime Minister if he could inform the House why the news of the summoning of the Conference was communicated to only a section of the morning Press, and if it was the case that the parties to the Conference were asked to keep the matter secret. Mr. Asquith said he very much regretted— no one more—that there had been any anticipa- tion of his announcement, which that House had the first title to hear. Neither he nor any- one for whom he was responsible made any communication of any sort or kind to the Press.

"WHAT AUTHORITY?" Mr. Ginnell (1.N., N. Westmeath) asked the Prime Minister what precedent and what autho- r* he had for advising the King to place himself at the head of a conspiracy—(Opposi- tion cries of "Order ")—to defeat the decisions of that House? The Speaker went on to the next business.

IN THE HOUSE OF LORDS. In the House of Lords, Lord Courtney put two questions to Lord Crewe. The first con- cerned Ministerial responsibility ; the second concerned Parliamentary control. It is not enough, he argued, for the Government to accept the wishes of the Sovereign. The advice must be Ministerial advice, and the Sovereign must "acquiesce." On this point the Prime Minister had omitted to make any statement. Secondly, the announcement as it stood amounted to "the supersession of Parliament," which ought to remain supreme over a body even so authori- tative as the Council of Eight. Would Lord Crewe reassure the House? Lord Crewe was most reassuring. lie could tell "the faithful guardian" of Parliamentary liberty that there had been 'no abrogation of Ministerial responsibility—no departure from constitutional practice." There "has not been—cannot be—any intention to supersede Parliament."

LABOUR PROTEST TO KING. A special meeting of the Labour party was held in the House of Commons immediately after the Prime Minister's announcement of the Conference, and after consideration of the situation an adjournment was made to enable the officers of the party to draft a'resolution. The meeting was resumed at 6.30, and the fol- lowing resolution was unanimously carried "The members of the Labour party having heard the Prime Minister's statement that the King has considered it right to summon a con- ference of representatives of British and Irish parties to discuss outstanding points on the problem of Irish government, and having been unable to express their opinions in the House of Commons itself owing to the way in which the statement was made, though strongly wish- ing for a permanent settlement, protest against such command having been issued regarding any matter of political controversy as being an undue interference on the part of the Crown and calculated to defeat the purposes of the Parliament Act; they further put on record their surprise that two of the representatives as,e practically rebels under arms against con- stituted authority, and regret this indication that in future an organisation of force is to be officially considered to be most effective in in- dustrial as well as in political disputes." It was decided that a copy of the resolution should be forwarded to the Prime Minister with a request that it be communicated to the King.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE CRY OF CIVIL WAR."

The Much-criticised King's Speech to the Conference.

When the Conference assembled on Tuesday at Buckingham Palace His Majesty addressed the members as follows: Gentlemen,—It is with feelings of satisfaction and hopefulness that I receive you here to- day, and I thank you for the manner in which you have responded to my summons. It is also a matter of congratulation that the Speaker has consented to preside over your meetings. My intervention at this moment may be re- garded as a new departure. But the excep- tional circumstances under which you are brought together justify my action. For months we have watched with deep mis- givings the course of events in Ireland. The trend has been surely and steadily towards an appeal to .force, and to-day the cry of civil war is on the lips of the most responsible and sober-minded of my people. We have in the past endeavoured to act as a civilising example to the world, and to me it is unthinkable, as it must be to you, that we should be brought to the brink of fratri- cidal strife upon issues apparently so capable of. adjustment as those you are now asked to consider, if handled in a spirit of generous compromise. .

My apprehension, in contemplating such a

MISS NELLIE MULLINS MARRIED.

Quiet Early-Morning Wedding at Trinity Church.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
CLEVER AMATEUR ACTRESS.

Early on Monday morning, and in the pres- ence of only a very few friends, who alone knew that the interesting ceremony was to take place, the marriage was solemnised by special licence at Holy Trinity Church, Warrington, of Mr. Robert Whittaker Budd and Miss Helen Mullins. Mr. Budd, who is brewer at Wilderspool Brewery, where he has been Mr. C. Garton Stones' chief lieutenant since Mr. Stone was appointed head brewer on the death of the late Councillor W. B. Roberts, is the son of Mr. and Mrs. Budd, of Blackpool, and has been

Garton Stone acted as bridesmaid. lfr. Kaleb Smith, jun.,..Liverpool, was best man._

. _ . The wedding breakfast was served at the Patten Arms, and the newly-married couple departed, amid the good wishes of their friends, almost immediately for North Wales, where the honeymoon is being spent The bride was mar- ried in a plain costume and hat, in which she also travelled to North Wales.

Miss Nellie Mullins, as she was popularly known, is an amateur actress and singer of conspicuous ability. Possessed of a light mezzo

It was to a large extent the fact of her popularity which induced her friends to advise her to have a wedding of the " quiet " kind, for there can be no doubt that interest in her marriage, had it become known generally, would have been so keen that public might have been on an embarrassing attention

KING CALLS CONFERENCE—continued.

dire calamity, is intensified by my feelings of attachment to Ireland, and of sympathy with her people, who have always welcomed me with warm-hearted affectioth Gentlemen, you represent in one form or another the vast majority of my subjects at home. You also have a deep ,interest in my Dominions over sea, who are scarcely less con- cerned in a prompt and friendly settlement of this question. ===== 0002601_19140725_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. ASQUITH ACCEPTS FULL RESPONSIBILITY.

The publication of the speech called forth some fiery criticism. It was assumed that His Majesty in referring to the "cry of civil war," referred to the Ulster partisans as the " most responsible and sober-minded of my people," and there were " questions" in the House on Wednesday. Mr. Asquith said: The speech was sent to me in the ordinary way by Ms Majesty the day before. I take the whole responsibility for it. The King left it at the discretion of the Con- ference to determine whether or not the Speech should be published, and the Conference unani- mously decided in favour of publication. His Majesty has throughout this matter fol- lowed the strictest constitutional precedent. He has not taken any step, from the beginning p to now, except in consultation with and on the advice of his Ministers. The statement was received in silence on the Liberal side of the House, but with cheers on the Opposition side.

TRADES COUNCIL AND LABOUR MEN. At a meeting of the Warrington Trades Coun- cil on Tuesday it was resolved: "That this Council heartily approve of the action taken by the Parliamentary Labour Party in protesting against the interyentions of the Crown in matters of legislation. Whilst we deplore the friction created over the Ulster crisis, and are n hopeful of an early settlement: we contend that Parliament should rely upon its own powers to enforce the collective will of the people, and at toh all teimreedsittaory opposea n extensio of prerogative powers.'

===== 0002601_19140725_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFERENCE FAILS.

. The Premier announced in the House of Com- mons yesterday that the Conference had failed, having been unable to agree upon the question of th 4 exclusion area. The Amending Bill will he taken on Tuesday.

• The Duke as a Shot. One experiences rather a sensation of sur- prise upon learning incidentally from a recent autobiography that the "Iron Duke" was a wild and most inaccurate shot. During a visit to Maresfield, the county home of the Shelleys, the hero of Waterloo, who was one of the shooting party, first wounded a re- triever, early one morning, then later in the day was unfortunate enough to pepper the keeper's gaiters; and just before the party broke UP in the afternoon he further distinguished himself by sprinkling the bare arms of an old woman who chanced to be washing clothes at her cottage window.

What Ruined Adam? A young English suffragette tells the follow- ing incident that happened at a meeting in the Scotch Highlands: Speeches had been made to a large crowd. Questions had been replied to, amid applause. Imbecile young men making remarks about minding babies and mending socks had been silenced. Then just as there was a temporary lull before the putting of a resolution, a great bucolic Scotch voice from the back of the crowd rasped slowly in with the inquiry, obviously the result of prolonged rumination: "Nina made a mess of Adam?".

===== 0002601_19140725_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
MISS NELLIE MULLINS.

soprano voice and an animated style, she originally appeared in the chorus in the Musi- cal Society's "Torn Jones." She was soon singled out for advancement, and when "San Toy" was produced she appeared as Dudley, with Mrs. Garton Stone in the title role. Her success was immediate and pronounced. In the " Dorothy " year she played Lydia to Mrs. Stone's Dorothy. -

Then the Dramatic Society entrusted her with the part of " Sunday " in the well-known play of that name, and Miss Mullins promptly made a fine reputation for herself as an actress pure and simple. Last year she was back again in the ranks of the Musical Society's Opera Company, and added considerably to her distinctions by the way she played the part of Mina in "'Miss Hook of Holland."

LOCAL WEDDING.

LEADBEATER-LOOKER.

A pretty wedding took place at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Chapel on Monday afternoon, when Miss Margaret -T. Looker, seventh daughter of the late Mr. William Looker, was married to Mr. Sydney C. Leadbeater, third son of Mr. T. C. Leadbeater, of Barnton, near Northwich. The bride, who was charmingly attired in white silk, wore a very pretty band-embroidered veil and a wreath of orange blossom, was given away by her stepfather, Mr. G. Gough. She carried aliouquet of white lilies. The bridesmaids—Miss nine Leadbeater (sister of the bridegroom), Miss Cissie Wright (niece of the bride)—wore champagne satin robes, with Tuscan hats, and Miss Gladys Peacock (niece of the bride) wore a pale blue satin robe with Tuscan hat. They carried bouquets of pink earnations. Miss Gladys Knowles (niece of the bride) was attired in cream silk and carried a basket of roses.

Mr. W. C. Leadbeater discharged the duties of best man and Messrs. F. Looker and S. Wright were the groomsmen. During the cere- mony, which was conducted by the Rev. H. Middlewood, Mr. Bromley played suitable music, including "The voice that breathed o'er Eden" and the Wedding March. After the ceremony the guests partook of the wedding breakfast at the Winmarleigh Cafe, and later in the evening the happy couple left for St. Annes-on-Sea. Many handsome and valuable presents were received.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

LIVERPOOL'S HOLIDAY ATTRAC- TION. Revival of "The Arcadians."

" The Well of Truth." There can be few, if any theatre-goers who are not conversant with the above story of a man who was thrown down a well for telling a lie! Should there be anyone ignorant of this, the man's name is " Simplici- tus." He appears in. Mr. Robert Courtridge's Company, in that fantastic musical play "The Arcadians," which will be the August Bank Holiday attraction at the Royal Court Theatre, Liverpool, for one week only. No one should fail to witness the eccentricities of Simplicitus.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
GIANTESS AT THE HIPPODROME.

The "greatest" turn at the Hippodrome is unquestionably that provided by Abomah, an African giantess, who is eight feet in height and weighs 24 stones. After delighting the audience with her singing of ragtime songs she makes a tour of the various parts of the house and greets a large proportion of the audience. Valvero entertains the audience in a delightful fashion with his lightning drawings, and Charles Grantley is quite popular as a very erratic comedian. Huley and Bent are smart comedians who have a host of clever gags and witty patter. Henry Helme, described as the French Alpine Vocalist, scores a decided success with his well- rendered songs, and Arthur Page sends the audience into uncontrollable fits of laughter with his Lancashire dialect comedy. Dorothy Meade has a sweet soprano voice which is heard to advantage in several well-chosen songs.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
Next Week's Show.

Clark's comedy cyclists will pay a first visit next week, and are booked to present the funni- est cycle act on the stage. Other turns will be: The Meymotts, "In the Future," a comedy playlet; the Bell Hattons, expert musicians; Fox and Melville, song illustrators; Housby and Rene, in "Fine Feathers"; Nella Dene, in song and dance; and the Three Veros, vocalists and dancers

===== 0002601_19140725_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA.

The principal films for Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday will be The Brass Bowl" and "Rebecca's Wedding Day," and for the re- mainder of the week The Fatal Enchantress" will occupy the premier position on a good bill.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
TANN ERS' STRIKE. Is There a Prospect of a Settlement? THE MAYOR'S ACTION.

Although the Mayor of Warrington's offer to again meet the employers was rejected on Thurs- day, the men's deputation yesterday met the Lord Mayor of Liveipool, who, we are informed, has seen the employers, and it is therefore to be hoped that a settlement is in progress. A prominent official of flue men, seen by "The Examiner" representative last night, said that meanwhile everything was in abeyance. The representatives of the men had met the Lord Mayor of Liverpool, but beyond that he could not say what had transpired. The meet- ing was an absolutely private orre.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYOR'S FRESII OFFER. He Again Meets With a Rebuff from

the Masters.

IN THE CAUSE OF THE FAMILIES.

The Mayor has once more offered his, services in the hope of a settlement of the strike, and on Wednesday the Town Clerk on his behalf sent the following letter, but it met with a tele- phonic reply that the masters did not see any advantage would accrue from such a conference as suggested, axt, the time was not opportune. The letter ran : •

Town Clerk's Office, Warrington,

22nd July, 1914.

Dear- Sir;.

I am asked' by His Worship the Mayor to write andi ask that he might be allowed to meet the master tanners with regard to the existing dispute. I may say His Worship has already ileen in communicatlon with Mr. Withinshaw thereon.

His Worship wishes it to be clearly under- stood that while in the general case he has no desire to interfere in trade disputes, yet he feels the present satiation is so serious; the families of the men being face to face with want, that he would not be carrying out the duties of his office as Mayor if lie did not: again try and see if some settlement could not be reached.

His Worship has cancelled an important engagement in order to be able to come to your meeting; and will be at his office at West-street all this afternoon. On receipt of a telephone message he will be pleased to come and meet your Federation at any time they may fix. Yours faithfully,

J. Lo N WHITTLE.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
H. Percy Densliam, Esq., Chairman or the Master Tanners' Federation.

THE MAYOR INTERVIEWED.

Last night in, an interview with our repre- sentative the Mayor said he was of the opinion that the time was opportune, and, personally he thought the time for action had passed and that the matter ought to have been settled long since.

A little bit of common sense was required, on which side. he would not say, but he would

say that in the interests of all the dispute ought to be settled. The Mayor also informed our representative that Councillor Roberts (chairman of the Education Committee), Mr. Plinston, Mr. Moore Murray, and himself were going to meet a deputation of the Trades and Labour Council this morning to discuss the question of feeding the children during the holidays.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
FEEDING OF STRIKERS' CHILDREN.

The Question Raised at the Education

Committee.

Incidentally-, the question of the feeding of necessitous school children during the holidays was raised at the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Tuesday. The School Management Committee minutes said The Director having reported that, in con- sequence of representations made to him, he had taken the usual steps to ascertain what children attending school are unable, by reason of lack of food, to take full advantage of the education provided for them, the committee carefully con- sidered the reports of the head teachers and the applications of parents, and it was resolved: (a) That the committee are of opinion that at present there is little evidence of pressing need for the provision of meals for school children; (b) That, as the committee cannot legally pro- vide food for school children during holidays, and the summer holidays commence on Friday, the 17th July, the Director be instructed to obtain the latest information in order to place the committee in a position to at once decide whether or not to put in operation the Educa- tion (Provigfon of Meals) Act, 1906; and to take such steps that, if necessary, the school canteen may be opened immediately after the holidays. Mr. E. Plinston said the General Purposes Committee had since endorsed that resolution, and regretted that it was the only method by which they could deal with the feeding of the school children, seeing that the holidays were upon them. They sincerely regretted that they could not put in operation the feeding of the children during the holidays, but it was the hope of that committee that steps would be taken to provide something in the way of sus- tenance for the children affected by this unfortu- nate dispute.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADES COUNCIL AND CONI- NIITFEE'S DECISION. Mr. Plinston Explains What Happened.

DEPUTATION SENT TO THE MAYOR. Some strong remarks were made at the meet- ing of the Trades Council on Tuesday night over the decision arrived at by the Education Committee in regard to feeding school children who required to be fed during the school holi- days. Mr. E. Bandies presided, and Mr. E. Plinston, the council's representative on the Education Committee, was present to explain the position. Mr. Greerley asked what was to be done to assist the tanners, who had made out a very genuine case. Every affiliated society should immediately organise to assist the pen on strike. In reply it was stated that the Executive Council had asked Mr. Plinston to move in the matter at the Education Committee.

MR. PLINSTON'S STATEMENT. Some Pointed Remarks. Mr, E. Plinston said he was glad that the question had been raised. A fortnight ago a deputation of the tanners waited on the Mayor and the Deputy Town Clerk, and raised the question of feeding the children. The Mayor suggested that the deputation should see the ' Director of Education. They did so, and after that one of the deputation remarked that there was too much red tape about the procedure. I think, added Mr. Plinston, there is far too much red tape about that particular Act of Parliament as well as about the methods of carrying out the provisions in all Acts of Par- liament. (Hear, hear.) As the result of the conversation with the tanners, the Director of Education saw me at the General Purposes Committee, and he obtained an instruction to at once put into operation the only method we have—to ask the teachers to be the judges and say whether the children were deserving cases and required feeding. A special meeting was held a week past on Monday, but prior to that a number of the men, representative of families, waited upon the Director and some of the officials and placed particulars before them. As a result it was stated at the special meeting of the General Purposes Committee that about 130 cases' would be required to be fed. The Committee found there was no nimteuadtcjohtiniinateinryfatchttoe, operate the Act in Warrington, and it was with e the knelv greatesttberer regret egtr great d e leaesrt town. I think it would be only pizeT.to feed the children, but where there is.sp provision you can only feed the children when the schools are open, and the schools were then about to close for the holidays.

Children Should Not Suffer. The first part of the resolution (quoted above) did not meet with my approval. I said that whatever might be the opinion about the dis- puta—in all disputes there is more than one

••

side of opinion—whatever opinion there might be, for or against the righteousness of the dis- pute, whether the men are absolutely in the wrong or not, and I say they are not, the chil- dren have absolutely no right to suffer, and in ray opinion we should be able to provide the children with the necessary food. I still maintain that the children should be fed. (Ap- plause.) _ . _

- The chairman and myself at the General Purposes Committee stated that one of the strongest appeals should be made to the public to enable us to feed the children, whether or not the schodls are open. Whether that is a matter to be taken up by the Mayor I cannot say. I regret the unfortunate position in which this Act of Parliament places us. Personally, from the way the men are fight- ing the strike, it appears to me there must be some righteousness in their dispute. (Applause.) I am prepared to do everything in my power to assist these men. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19140725_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
AFRAID OF SURCHARGE.

Mr. C. Dukes: Personally, I place no great importance upon the statement of the Educa- tion Committee that they are debarred from feeding the children during the holidays. Other Education Committees have got over that difficulty. The members may have been sur- charged, but they have never paid the sur- charges. It seems as if our committee are afraid of surcharge. West Ham and Bradford offer typical examples of what can be done. On the oCcasion of the carters' strike and Fletcher's strike, when the schools were open, they did nothing to put the Act into operation, and the poverty was acute. I state here dis- tinctly that some members of the Education Committee do not believe that the children should be fed during industrial disputes, and these members have distinctly expressed that view.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
" Said Most Deliberately."

It has been said most deliberately that the men are no more entitled to receive support out lof the public purse than the employers. The workers are ratepayers and greatly outnumber the employers, yet it is made out that the em- ployers alone should control the public purse. (Applause.) Because a few bodies are divided in their religious opinions they claim representation on the local Education Authority, whilst the men in their combined number could claim repre- sentation on a basis of 10,000 members they are only allowed to be represented by one man, and he is a co-opted member. (" Shame! ") I say it is high time we set to work to re- move the disgrace, and the best way is to fight every man who is hostile to the workers when he comes out for re-election. (Applause.) NO ONE SPOKE. Mr. Plinston : When a chance was given in the Committee to say what some of them seem to have said outside not one spoke. No one ex- cept myself was prepared to make a statement. (".Shame.")

A delegate proposed that they should write the Mayor asking him to start an appeal. Mr. Plinston suggested that they should send a deputation of three members. This was agreed to, and Messrs. Grearley, Dukes, and Berry were appointed the deputation.

A STRIKE "SONG."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
Tanners and Sympathisers Appear in

the Police Court.

BOUND OVER FOR SIX MONTHS.

Singing a strike " song," which was alleged to have contained the phrase " Where the scabs are there will we go," was mentioned as Con- stituting a breach of the peace in a case in which two tanners and two sympathisers appeared in the Warrington Police Court on Thursday. The magistrates were Mr. J. Rymer Young (in the chair), Alderman James Evans and Dr. Naden. Brought up on summons were William Moly- neaux, joiner, 346, Knutsford-road ; James Brad- shaw, tanner, 30, Rock-road; Thomas Collins, tanner, Raddon-court, who were charged with disorderly conduct in Lower Wash-lane; and Fred Willoughby, moulder, 28, Sandhurst-street, was charged with a like offence in Lockett-street. All, it was said, took place on the night of 4th July. The four men pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable said as the justices were aware, there was a strike of tanners, and a man named Sykes, living at Lower Wash-lane, had kept at his work, and that had acted as an in- centive to the defendants to become disorderly at the house of Sykes.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
A WOMAN WITNESS.

Alice Sykes, wife of John Sykes, a tanner's engineer, of 13, Lower Wash-lane, said on the evening of Saturday, 4th July, Molyneaux opened the garden gate, and, accompanied by his dog, walked in. Her husb_and ordered him out. He went, and started to sing a song about "scabs." There were four men, and she heard Bradshaw sing. Bradshaw: I never was able to sing in my life.

Witness, continuing, said later Molyneaux re- turned with a crowd of children, who sang the " scab " song. About midnight she heard a crash of glass, and found a large stone (pro- duced) in the sitting-room. The window was broken, and a,chair damaged. Molyneaux was the leader of the men, who seemed to have had some drink. She considefed that they were very disorderly. Molyneaux : Was I disorderly?—You were singing the " scab " song, and that was dis- orderly. The Chief Constable: You have been annoyed' through your husband working ?—Yes. When Collins was served with a summons, what did he do with it?—He pasted it on the garden gate. (Slight laughter in court.)

"FRIENDLY PERSUASION." John Sykes said he worked at Parkinson's Tannery, and lived at 13, Lower Wash-lane. He was still at work, and had been much annoyed through strikers and other workers referring to him as a "scab." When he told Molyneaux to clear out of the garden he went out, and started singing about a "scab." This he kept up for ten minutes. Witness went to the Police Sta- tion to report the case, and when he came back he found that Molyneaux had a band of chil- dren singing the "scab" song. the comeeh children outn ta with v the g?..... other Did I molest you F—l do not know swirintr about you molesting me; you were the leader. peeDpidlelenndot ask preventveynotu never spoke a word about that. The Chief Constable: If Molyneaux was ask- ing you not to go back to work so that the chil- dren of Warrington might be fed, did he come to you in the right way?—No, I have never been asked in the right way.

IN THE COURTS.

INCIDENT ON THE LINE.

Two AVarrington Fruit Salesmen and

the L. and N.W. Co.

• WERE THEY TRESPASING? The story of an early morning " scene " at Bank Quay Station was narrated at the War- rington Police Court on Tuesday. Councillor H. Roberts presided over the Benet. Hugh McDermott, fruit salesman, 48, Bewsey- street, and Harold Kitchen, fruit salesman, 41, Bewsey-street, were charged with having tres- passed on the L. and N.-W. line at Bank Quay Station at 4.45 on the morning of 10th June, and did thus expose themselves to danger. Both said they had trespassed, but had been in no danger. Mr. Tait, Euston, for the company, said Messrs. McDermott and Kitchen were fruit salesmen, and were in the habit of attending the market at Manchester. They came to the sta- tion at 4.45 a.m. on the 10th of June to catch the 5 a.m. train for Manchester. They did not use the usual passenger entrance, but crossed over the horse dock, then they got on the metals to cross to the platform, and as trains were shunting, placed thentselves in serious danger. Inspector Nash, who was in charge of the shunting operations, spoke to them, and they refused to go back, and Nash had to stop the engine shunting to avoid a serious accident. McDermott then threatened Nash.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
INSPECTOR'S STORY.

William A. Nash said he was a traffic inspec- tor, and he saw the men come on the' line about 4.45 a.m. on the 10th June. They came down by the horse dock instead of using the usual pas- senger entrance. He had to bring the shunting engine to a stop. He told them that they were trespassing, but they refused to go back. McDermott said he had gone so far, and he would complete his journey to the Manchester platform. He also said, added the Inspector, "that if I laid hands on him he would knock me so that my mother would not knovi me." McDermott: Didn't I tell you that you were a bully?—You might have said that. Didn't you put your hands on me?—' did. James Leathers, foreman shunter, said at the request of Nash he stopped the shunting engine. Kitchen: There was no engine about at all ! There was, the,same engine I had worked wnith all night.

DEFENDANTS' VERSION. Mr. Kitchen said he could understand, if they had been loafers, the company taking action, but the company got out of them hundreds of pounds every year. They had been in the habit of going through the same way every morning, and had never once been cautioned. Mr. McDermott said Nash came up to them in a- bullying manner, and they had resented it. He had never before been cautioned. The magistrates asked both to par9s. costs. Mr. Tait asked for an advocate's fee. The Chairman (Councillor Roberts): We can- not allow it. Mr. Tait: Then we must allow trespassers to get themselves killed.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (iiith—e-Cl;E"tir), Alderman Bol- ton, Alderman Smethurst, Alderman James Evans, Dr. Joseph, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. T. Locker, G. Pilling, S. Wright and W. Furness. _

An Early Morning Visitor.—Edward Char- nock, Pinner's Brow, was charged with loitering with intent in Vernon-street Tannery early on Sunday morning.—P.C. Hayes said that he found accused in the yard of the tannery at 4.30 on Sunday morning, and accused said that he had been asked to go to work there. Charnock had to climb over a nine-foot gate to get into the yard, and as he (the officer) was suspicious about his story he took the man into custody.— Accused was ordered to pay ss. and no costs.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

Before Councillor H. Roberts (in the chair), Mr. C. Jackson Holmes and Mr. T. Locker. Helped the Poor Box.—Patrick Doyle, a miner, who said he came from Prescot, was charged with being drunk in Horsemarket-street on Monday night.—The Chief Constable said if the police had not taken care of accused he would have lost a considerable sum of money he had in his possession< Doyle was discharged and agreed to put ss. into the poor box. An Array of Offences.—John William Stock- ton, Of 11, Gandy-street, an iron-worker, had four charges to answer: (1) That he was drunk and disorderly; (2) that he did damage to win- dows at 11, Gandy-street, to the extent of 45.; (3) that he broke into a penny-in-the-slot_sas meter and stole from it is. Ild, in copper, and (4) that he had neglected to maintain his chil- dren. He pleaded guilty to all charges.—Detec- tive Clough said that he and Inspector Wain- wright, of the, N.S.P.C.C., went to see accused. He behaved like a madman and they had a ter- rific struggle with him.—Mr. A. Browne, prose- cuting in the cruelty case, said Stockton could earn good wages and often was sent to work all over the country. In seven weeks he had given his wife only 325. On Saturday he came home drunk, threw things at his wife, and bundled her and the children into the street, where they had to remain the greater part of the night. He had written stating that he had been hurt at Newcastle, but inquiries showed it was not so.— Accused was fined in all £2 15s. and costs.—The Chief Constable: Are we to give him time to pay ?—Councillor Roberts: We have sufficient confidence in you, Mr. Chief, that you will use reasonable discretion.

WEDNESDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Messrs. J. W. Hayward, C. Jackson Holmes, and W. T.

"Trying It On."—Jane Jones, no fixed abode, was remanded for seven days on a charge of stealing a dress skirt value 6s. lid, from the pawnshop of Mr. Harry Houghton, 30, Mersey- street.—The Chief Constable said the woman stole the article from the door of the shop, and was seen pinning the skirt on in Bolton's Yard. She tried to make out to Mr. Houghton that she wanted to purchase the skirt and was trying, it on. The skirt was a very bad fit for her. •

A Boy's Lesson.—John William Richardson (17), 4, Pinshop-yard, was charged with steal- ing a pair of gold spectacles value 10s. 6d., the property of William Morris, 63, London-road, Stockton Reath.—The Chief Con-stable said the boy took the spectacles from a coat pocket in the engine-room at Dallam Forge, wherele was employed. The boy admitted the , offence when taxed by Acting-Sergeant Edwards, and said he had taken the pebbles out and had sold the frame in town. The pebbles and frame had been recovered.—The boy's father said he could not itcount in any way for his son's conduct.—The boy was bound over for six months, the fattier going surety Mr him.—The Mayor (to prisoner) : Take a lesson from this. If you do not, there is no telling how you will go on. You may become a little gaolbird.

WATER POLO.

Warrington's Further Successes.

Warrington met Liverpool away on Monday night and were poorly represented, being short of Woods, Pepper and S. Roberts. However, the reserves played well, and Warrington won by six goals to two, the scorers being C. Turner (2), J. Graham (2), S. Ince and H. Sisson. Jolly, in goal, played a good game. Warrington met Altrincham at Altrincham on Wednesday, and won a close game by five goals to three. The scorers were: C. Turner (2), J. Graham (2), and S. Ince. On Thursday Warrington play Woolton (the League leaders), and on this night there will be a 50 yards handicap, open to lady residents in the borough. Three suitable prizes will be given, and also a commencement, will be made the third round of the Work& Competition.

CHILDREJI AT PICTURE SYOWS.

Subject Again Discussed at the Education

===== 0002601_19140725_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
Committee.

MAYOR DEFENDS WATCH COMMITTEE.

The question of children attending picture shows came up again at the Warrington-Educa- tion Committee on Tuesday. The decision of the Watch Committee was announced and the minutes of the Medical Inspection Sub-com- mittee stated :

"A letter was submitted from the Town Clerk, and it was noted that the Health Committee had approved the resolutions of this committee with reference to the attendance of school chil- dren at cinematograph exhibitions, with the following alterations, viz.:—ln paragraph 1: That the hour of 8.15 p.m. shall be altered to 9 p.m. In paragraph 4: That the words 'chil- dren under 14' be substituted for the words the audience.'

" With further reference to this matter, the committee -noticed with great regret that the Watch Committee had not adopted the recom- mendations of the Health Committee and this committee contained in paragraph 2, with refer- ence to the exclusion of school children during school hours, and it was resolved: That the Watch Committee be strongly urged to recon- sider the matter with a view to including the recommendation in their regulations."

ment during school hours, to be just they should be excluded from other forms.

He told the Education Committee candidly that he should vote against 'restrictions being placed on one particular form of entertainment to the exclusion of others. The Waten Com- mittee would not allow one particular form of amusement to be punished. He suggested that the Medical Inspection and the Health Committees should modify their re- quests and then the Watch Committee would deal with them in a manner • that would be reasonable and just, and not put the screw on unfairly.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COMMITTEE'S VIEWS..

Mr. H. KINGDON moved approval of the min- utes. The Health Committee not only approved of the action- of the Medical Inspection Com- mittee, but said they' would render assistance by recommending the proposals to the Watch Com- mittee.

The Watch Committee, on the other hand, had not passed the resolutions in their entirety, and he should like to urge the extreme desira- bility of any matter relating to the health of the children of the borough, which had the joint approval of the Health and Medical In- spection Committees by the most cordial support of the Watch Committee. (Hear, hear.) The resolutions had not been taken in hand in a light spirit, and there was a great deal of discus- sion about them.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Disagreeable Question."

The Medical Inspection Committee had the benefit of the experience of the Chairman of the Health Committee, and it was felt that the regulations proposed were for the benefit of the children. The Watch Committee felt that their position was rather a disagreeable one—that the Inspection and Health Committees proposed the medicine and left the Watch Committee to ad- minister it. (Laughter.) But that was not the fault of the Health and Inspection Committees. They would be pleased to do the forcible feeding—(more laughter)— if they had the power, but they had not, and their whole limit was to form the recommenda- tions and pass them on for .the approval of the Watch Committee.

Liberty of the Subject.

From reports in the Press of the discussion in the Council, he saw that a good deal was made about the liberty of the subject. That liberty of the subject gave a sufficient reason for interfer- ing for the safety of the majority. In certain circumstances liberty became licence, and the argument that one interfered with the liberty of the subject when he took steps to prevent the spread of infectious disease was one which would not carry much weight. After the time and trouble the two committees had Spent in considering the matter they ought to receive the support of the Watch Committee. He asked any member who was in a position to exercise an influence in the direction he had indicated to do FO, so that his committee's recom- mendations might be carried. Mr. E. PLINSTMf seconded the adoption of the minutes.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
WATCH COMMITTEE'S POSITION.

Alderman JAMES EVANS said that if the two special committees—the Health and Medical In- spection—did not understand the question, one was led to conclude that the Watch Committee did not understand it. The Watch Committee ought to give the two committees he had named some credit at least for having given the recom- mendations fuller consideration than'thertbuld have done. He did not know what course would be taken now, except one which suggested itself to his mind, and that was this The recommendations had been passed in all sincerity, and the Watch Committee must take the responsibility for the results which would undoubtedly follow if they rejected them.

The subject was a difficult one to handle, but a good deal could be done on lines of prevention, And a great deal of suffering and inconvenience avoided.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0220.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAYOR'S REPLY.

The MAYOR said, as chairman of the Watch Committee, he was compelled to reply. • The Watch Committee and the Council had fully considered the matter, and the Watch Com- mittee's resolution had met with the confidence of.the Town Couneil, Who declined to accept Mr.. Kingdon's recommendation. The Watch Committee, he Could assure all, had discussed the matter very fully and very carefully, and it was only by a majority that they .declined to adopt the whole of the recom- mendations. They thought—and he was in full accord with them—that if chililren were to be excluded from one particular form of entertain-

RUGBY. INTERMEDIATE N.U. LEAGUE.

Rapid Progress Now Being Made.

TEAMS WHO lIAVE JOINED. BY " SC R U M."

Good tidings continue to reach me about the formation of the new Intermediate N.U. League and the success which I predicted is now assured. One felt from the start that the enthu- siastic way in which Councillor Osborne intro- duced the idea, and the equally enthusiastic manner in which the promoters set to work, would end in capital results, and so it has proved. The Secretary has kindly furnished me with a list of teams who have joined, and these are as follows : _

===== 0002601_19140725_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
"UNFORTUNATE PART."

Mr. PLiNsTox said the unfortunate part was that the Watch Committee had thrown out the most important recommendation—that children should be prevented from attending such places when they were not permitted to attend 5611501. The Mayor had given a reason, but he (the speaker) did not think the Watch Committee should have done so, as it would be remem- bered that children excluded from sc-hool did not go to the theatres or the Hippodrome, but to the picture houses. They did not wish to be unfair to anyone, but the first object was to protect the children.

_ Councillor BaoAninansx, said it was not fair to condemn the Watch Committee, as on the Health Committee the proposal was only carried by one vote.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WEAK POINT.

_ The CHAIRMAN (Councillor H. Roberts) said it would be a good thing if the Medical Inspection Committee took some notice of the weak point of the repolution. There was more than a sug- gestion the last time they discussed the matter; a statement was made that it was a little in- vidious to pick out of the various houses of entertainment in the town one particular sec- tion. Some objections would be removed if the principle of the recommendations could be made to apply to all praces of entertainment under the same conditions. (Hear, hear.) Mr. R. T. FAIRCLOUGH said that it was right to exclude children from the places of amusement when there was infectious disease in the town. But were all the children to be excluded- when the town was free from disease?

The DIRECTOR OF EDUCATION: The children would be at school then.

INFECTIOUS DISEASES.

Mr. FAIRcLouGa But there would be some children—those who do not attend the ,local elementary schools—who would not be at school. I know, a mother who would not take a child to the fair because there was infectious disease in the town, but she does not live in Warring- ton, but is here on a visit. If the Medical Officer certifies that there is no disease, the reso- lution ought to be rescinded. The CHAIRMAN : You don't appreciate the. point. A number of children are exempt from attend- ance at scitool beCause they are suffering from illness or are contact cases. On the advice of the Medical Officer, these children are prevented from attending school. Yet they are seen to go to the cinema—l mean kinema--(laughter)— shows, and become a possibility—being contact cases—of spreading the disease, with a view to preventing which their compulsory exclusion from school is enforced.

Mr. FAIRCLOUGH I quite appreciate it. The CHAIRMAN: If the arrangement could be made to apply all round there would be no valid objections, and it would remove all, sense of in- justice. (Hear, hear.) Mr. FAIRCLOIIIB/1 PM:lite agree. MAYOR'S "FALLACIOUS ARGUMENT."

Mr. KINGDON, in reply, said that when they first began to discuss the matter he stated that, if it was the wish of the committee, the- regula- tion could apply to other forms of amusement in addition to pictute shows. He could not help thinking that the Mayor's argument was an. extremely fallacious- one.

He practically said: "We quite agree with what you propose, but because YOU are half- hearted and don't go the whole way, we won't have anything to do with it." The Watch Com- mittee could have said: "We approve of the regulations when applied to all places of amuse- ment in the town." The Medical Inspection Committee were only making suggestions.—They had no power to carry them out. The MAYOR: We don't put our suggestions on the house tops.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
APPLIED TO ALL PLACES.

Mr. KINGDON : So far as my committee is con- cerned, there will be no difficulty in suggesting that the regulations be applied to all houses of amusement. Personally, I have always been in favour of that being done, but it was felt that we should start with the picture houses, because they came more seriously under our notice in connection with the exclusfon of children than the other places did. I suppose I may take it, after what the Mayor has said, that if w,e elude the other places we shall have his cordial support. The MAYOR: I don't say I could pledge the Watch Committee. I shall support their resolu- tion as it now stands. The minutes were adopted.

AMATEUR BOXING.

Results of the Bouts at the Old English Fair.

The results of the boxing contests at the Old English Fair will be found below. The referee was Dick Walsh, and the hon. secretary and M.O. Wm. W. Whittle.

On Saturday night a special contest took place between Owen Moran (13olton) and Kid Lees (Royton), eight two-minute rounds being fought. Moran led in the earlier rounds by use of a good left and eventually proved the winner on points, though Lees was very game.

lit. 121 b. Open competition..-. Combatants: Peers, Elliot, Earl, Macdonald, Ellison, Glynn, Shaw, Lythgoe, Dell, Connor, Simister, Morris, Warren.

Ix the final, Nipper Earl beat George Warren by a knock-out in the fifth round. The winner is to be complimented on his victory, as it Was his first appearance in 'a competition.

9st. 41b. Open Competition.—Combatants: Lee, Stockton, Webb, Longshaw, Forest, Riley, Brit- ton, Delenzo, Riley, Hewitt. In the final, Johnny Hewitt beat Charlie Forest by a knock-out in the seventh round.

In these competition bouts three rounds of three minutes each were fought, except in the final, when eight rounds of two minutes each were fought.

Some good exhibition contests were fought by the following: Johnny Gordon v.. Tom Thomas, Joe Durham v. Johnny Hewitt, Packey Ford v. Young Fogg, Charlie Forest v. Morky Lee. These bouts consisted of four rounds of two minutes each.

A medal offered for competition among Boy Scouts was won by Monks, of the Ist Warring- ton Troop.

On Saturday evening Young Carey, who is only about six years old, gave a capital exhibi- tion of club swinging.
