• t DISTRICT MEWS

STOCKTON HEATH

St. Thomas' School Managers.:—ln place of Mr. Henry Greenall, who is leaving Hill Cliffe, and the late Mr. William Richardson, the managers of St. Thomas' Schools, at a meeting on Tuesday, decided to appoint Messrs. T. J. Lee and James Richardson, jun., as managers.

Safer Than Ever.—" We shall feel safer than ever now," was the remark of the Chairman of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday, when the Chairman of the Fire Appliances Committee, Councillor McDermid, reported that Messrs Greenell, Whitley and Co.'s new motor fire engine had arrived.

Police Station Decoration.—The painting of the interior of the police station is to cost £72 10s., and at a meeting of the Cheshire Police Committee at Chester on Saturday the tender of Messrs. S. and' F. Acton, Warrington, was accepted. There were three tenders presented, and this was the lowest:

2s. 6d. for ls.—The local councillors feel some- what irritated by the fact that it had cost the Council 2s. 6d. to 3s. 6d. to take proceedings against a resident at Stockton Heath who was only fined is., including costs, for having allowed his chimney to be on fire.—At the meet- ing of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday the Clerk said it seemed farcical to go to that ex- pense for such a small penalty. He had in- structed the inspector to report any further cases to the committee before taking action.

Convenience Question.—The question of the provision of a public convenience is still before the Ship Canal Company. The Clerk to the Parochial Committee replied to a query from Councillor Rose on Tuesday that the Council had had the matter before them for some time now, and the ,Chairman remarked that the company were not very active in• some matters, as, for instance, he had been bothering them for two months about a certain thing and had only received an answer the previous week. The Clerk also stated that he had 'received no reply about the hoardings, except what he had reported some time ago.

Band of Hope Trip.—The 18th annual trip of the Hill Clifte and Stockton Heath United Band of Hope took place on Monday, when the party was conveyed to Warrington by tram, catching an early train from the Central Station to Southport. Arrived here, the members were served with lunch at Baths Cafe, after Thich the children quickly dispersed in search of the various pleasures of the beach. The new bath- ing lake proved an attraction, and many-cst the adults drove to Church Town Gardens and other places of interest in the district. Tea was served early at the Baths Cafe. After visiting the arcades in the evening, the party returned home well satisfied with their day's pleasure. Mr. G. Whitfield, secretary, had charge of the arrange- ments.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
SWING BRIDGE LIGHTS.

Parochial Committee and the Ever-

green Topic.

The swing bridge lighting was again the chief shbject at the Parochial Committee on Tuesday; when Councillor Pilling presided. There were also present Councillors H. .3. Westbrook, F. Pearson, J. McDermid, J. Whitley, F. Pose, J. Hallows, C. W. Hallows, Messrs. A. S. Wright (surveyor), G. F. Ashton (clerk), and E. Brad-, shaw (clerk to the Parish Council). The Lighting Committee had expressed .the opinion that in view 'cif the Mani coinpliiints received as to the alleged insufficient lighting of the bridge, arrangements should .be made, if possible, tor a hanging electric light on the bridge.

Councillor Whitley, who had .been appointed to approach the electricity department of the Warrington ,Corporation ou the matter, reported that he had interviewed Mr. Mathias, the electrical engineer for Warrington, who informed him that the electrical current was cut off at midnight. In reply-to the query as to whether it was possible tor a storage battery to be provided Mr. Mathias said it would be a very expensive and complicated affair, and he could not see the justification for installing one. Apart from those questions the Corporation had not at preSent powers to supply electric light outside the borough. It would, therefore, be wise to defer the matter until there was a better prospect of success.

The Chairman: We shall have to wait until they ket an order from the Board of Trade to serve the district.

- Mr. Whitley: They are seeking powers to light several- parishes. Mr. Mathias asked, "Are you at Stockton Heath against us?" and I said, "No, we are not." He then asked me if we were going to support the Corporation in their application, and I said, " That is a ques- tion.. We are not ,going against you, and I think you may take it that we should support you if we got reasonable terms."

The Chairman: The Committee wanted to be on the safe side. They would not say they would support the application, but they would ' not offer opposition. Mr. Whitley: Mr. Mathias agreed with me as to the present method of lighting, that the light of the incandescent gas burner on the Warring-, ton side of the bridge was almost obliterated by the standard erected just behind it, and a dark shadow was thrown right over the bridge. If two lights, like those on the Stockton Heath side, were provided, there would be no need for further provision. There was no further discussion on the matter, which was deferred.

PENKETH.

WEDDING OF MISS M. IRELAND.

A Very Pretty Ceremony.

The marriage of Mr. T. H. Elliott; son of Mr. T. Elliott, of Bradford, Yorkshire, and 'Miss J. Jackson, the adopted daughter of Mr. and Mrs. S. Ireland, Heath-road, Penketh, locally known as Miss Minnie Ireland, was solemnised on Thursday afternoon at the Penketh Wesleyan Chapel. The officiating minister was the Rev. J. Gunnell.

The bride was drdsed in white draped satin caught up with sprays of orange blossom over a lace underskirt. Her train was hand-em- broidered and trimmed with orange blossom. A tulle fall and wreath pf orange blossom, with a bouquet of lilies, white roses, sweet peas, and smilax completed a charming toilette. Miss Maggie Routledge acted as bridesmaid, and wore dress of white-corded silk, trimmed with tiny pearl ornaments, a black hat with a large plutne, and carried a shower bouquet of dark red roses and smilax.

Mr. Willis, of Bradford acted as a very capable best man. The bride's mother looked well in black silk, and carried a bouquet of "pink sweet peas. The bridegroom's mother, dressed in grey, carried mauve sweet peas. After the wedaing a reception was held in the schoolroom, and about fifty friends at- tended. After this the happy couple left for a tour in North Wales. The bride's travelling costume was Saxe blue, with a hat to match, trimmed with cherries.

• Presents numbering about fifty were received, both beautiful and serviceable.

MR. UT. G. DYKES MARRIED.

The wedding was solemnised last week of Miss Elsie Davis, youngest daughter of Mr. and Mrs. James Davis, of Millfield House, Newburn, near Newcastle-on-Tyne, and Mr. William Gillespie Dykes, second son of Mr. and Mrs. W. Dykes, of Hale. The service was fully choral, and was officiated over by the Revs. H. G. A. Throckley and E. J. Lynton, of Newcastle. c The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in shell pink satin, with silver-and- pearl embroidered court train. She wore a Brus- sels net veil mounted on lace, and orange blossorn, and carried a sheaf of Harissii lilies. Her gold wristlet watch Was the gift of the bridegroom. The dresses of the first two were of pale blue crepe de chine, with pale satin sashes and black Tagel hats. All wore pendants and chains, the gifts of the bride- groom, and carried bouquets of pink carnations. Mr. James White, of Manchester, acted as best man, and Mr. B. Glover as groomsman. After the reception, the newly-married couple left Newcastle for the North of Scotland, where the honeymoon is being spent. Many useful and valuable presents were re- ceived, including one from the Newburn Wes- jeyan Sunday school, with which Miss Davis' has been connected since childhood, and one from the employes of Spencer's Steel Wogs.

TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

A specially-designed electric-light bulb, con- structed in accordance with the plans of Mar- coni, was illuminated by wireless waves supplied from a 100-h.p. Plant six miles distant, recently. The success of the experiment, which is not yet adapted to commercial use, is a forerunner, ac- cording to Mr. Marconi, of the use of wireless in both lighting and beating houses, as well as for giving a means of supplying power to aeroplanes in flight.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
Fashioning a Car Body in Two Blows.

Most modern motor-car bodies are made from pressed steel, stamped to the desired shape (says "Popular Mechanics "). A stamping press built in America and now on exhibition in England is so large that the body of a touring car can be fashioned complete in two impressions. More than 50 men can find standing room in and upon this big machine, which, by means of a double- action toggle devise, is able to exert a pressure of 1,500 tons.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
The Tower of Babel.

In the course of excavations upon the site of Babylon, the German Orient Society (says the " Times") claim to have discovered traces of the Tower of Babel. The excavators found a colos- sal staircase of three flights, which is supposed to have led to the first storey of the tower. They have also unearthed the four great gates of the town wall built by Nebuchadnezzar's father, Nebupolassar, and have found traces of temples and walls belonging to the town built opposite Assur, on the farther bank of the Tigris, by King Tukultininib, who reigned 1300 B.C.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
" Kubisagari ": A Strange Disease.

One of the -strangest diseases is known as kubisagari. It is common in Northern Japan and in a small distria on the Franco-Swiss frontier, hut unknown elsewhere. Its symptoms are a sudden paralysis of certain muscles, loss of vision and vertigo. The attacks last about ten minutes and continue from May to November, recovery always coming in the autumn. Noth- ing is known about its cause, though Dr. P. L. Couchaud describes in the " Presse Medicale " a microbe he found in the cerebro-spinal fluid and the milk of persons suffering from the disease. The name kubisagari Ls Japanese, and means " drophead."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
Interesting Ruined. Town.

_ Among the many ruined cities of Mexico dat- ing back to antiquity is Mitla, of unusual in- terest to the sightseer, The name is a contrac- tion or corruption of the word Mictlan, mean- ing "the place of death." The modern village and the ancient ruins occupy the centre of an arid plain or valley, surrounded on all sides by equally arid hills, on the highest summit of which are the well-preserved remains of a great fortress. Nearly in the centre of the plain five great groups of buildings stand out. Many of them are now nearly shapeless mounds and masses of ruins. Two great groups, however, are in comparatively perfect preservation.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
Verona,.

Veronal has been known as a drug since 1904, when it was put on the market as a proprietary medicine. It is a drug which often loses its power with use, and it is this feature, which is common also to most 'other sleeping medicines, which makes it so-dangerOus unless taken under medical advice—without which it should never be taken at all. Although veronal appears now on the second schedule of the Poisons Act, its purchase is practically unrestricted, and chem- ists are at liberty to supply anybody with it provided the name of the drug, the name and address of the seller, and the word "Poison" be printed on the labels. Medical opinion is strongly in favour of a restriction of the sale of the dangerous drug.

Antiquity cf Umbrellas. As a shade from the sun, the umbrella seems to have had its origin in the East, in very re- mote times. We see it in the sculptures and paintings of Egypt, in the sculptures of Nineveh, Persepolis, and Assyria, and it appears to have penetrated to India. Judging from the sculp- tures, it is probable that the king alone carried it in Assyria, Nineveh and Persepolis; in Egypt and India, the privilege was extended to per- sons of the highest rank. In shape it was similar to those in use at the present day, but whether it admitted of being closed is doubt, ful. Its plainness was relieved by tassels, and there was generally a flower or some such orna- ment on the top.

An Interesting Relic. An interesting memorial of Canadian history, in the form of the arch and sidestones of the gateway of the Chateau de Brouage, Brittany, France, where Champlain was born, has been offered to the City of Montreal by Doctor Finley, president of the University of New York, who telegraphed the offer to W. D. Lighthall, K.C., president of the Montreal Antiquarian Society. The historic interest attliched to the gateway makes the gift a most valuable one. Samuel de Champlain, governor of the first French settlers in, Lower Canada, was born in 1567, and died in Canada in 1635, returning from England, where he was set free in 1632, after his capture by the Kirk brothers. Dr. Finlby intends later to pre- sent Montreal with another Champlain relic— the stone top of the wall 'of the old Brittany chateau.

A Difficult Legal Problem.

A most difficult legal problem will shortly come before the Viennese Courts for solution. A woman shot at her lover with murderous intent, but without inflicting the slightesfinjqry, since the gunsmith who sold her the revolver had pru- dently charged it with blank cartridges. She has been arrested on the charge of attempted murder, and admits her intention to kill, but the Austrian lawyers appear to be in consider- able doubt as to whether the chaigelln succeed, as the prisoner was in the circumstances unable to carry out her intention, The Case raises a very nice point of law; can a person be convicted of "shooting with intent to kill" under condi- tions in which it is impossible To carry out that intention? There is certainly room for much ingenious argument on both sides.

The Solemnity of Cricket.

The real cricket crowd (says Mr. Clarence Rook) is different from the football crowd, the baseball crowd, or any other crowd that takes its excitement in a gulp. A.football match lasts an hour and a half, and is exciting all the time; a baseball match splits the throlits of the par- tisans; polo is a swift and swirling sight; even golf, it seems, has its excitement. But for pure solemnity take me to Lord's—where the serious men come up in the afternoon to sit and smoke and watch a bit of the game they know, a game that may go on with those leisurely intervals for three days. lam quite sure that a German or a Frenchman or an American from anywhere west.of Massachusetts would be puzzled at the dullness of the game, though somehow the cricket pitch has been rolled round the English world from India to Fiji, Australia, South Africa..

A PURE FOOD. For the fifth successive year " ATORA " Re- fined Beef Suet has been awarded the Certificate of the Incorporated Institute of Hygiene for the highest Standard of Quality. Although " ATORA " contains no preservative, it keeps sweet for months. No chopping required; goes nearly twice as far as raw Suet. Your grocer sells it—Shredded (coated with rice flour) for puddings and pastry and in blocks for frying and cooking. . As': for " ATORA," refuse substituted brands

THE 'EXAMINER. SATURDAY. JULY 25, 1914.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
THE pßiNcEss pOCANONTAS.

AN ANCIENT ROMANCE RECALLED,

A Story of John Smith and the Early Virginian Settlers.

BY ARTHUR

At Gravesend the other day the American Ambassador unveiled memorial windows to a certain Pocahontas, who was laid to rest within the church nearly three centuries ago. That a Princess of an American-Indian tribe should have been found at Gravesend in the days of the Stuarts is a thing noteworthy enough in itself; that we should be doing homage after three centuries suggests that her story is re- moved from the ordinary. To successfully introduce the Princess Poca- hontas, it is necessary to first bring into notice a very remarkable Englishman, who bore the very sensible name of John Smith, and who made it known in four continents.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW JOHN SMITH SAW LIFE.

- John Smith (his parents could have had no intuition of his future when they made him plain John) was born at Willoughby, Lincoln- shire, in 1580. When sixteen years old, his father having died, he decided to go to sea. His guardians thought differently, and bound him apprentice to a merchant in Lynn. But business appears to have been much too slow a thing for the quick wits of John Smith, junior, and he decided to try the life of a soldier.

Hereupon began his adventures, and they came thick and fast. He travelled first to France with a son of Lord Willoughby, and did his first fighting under Henry IV. His next sphere was in the Low Countries, then Scotland (the story need not be told in detail), and then to Willoughby. Here he lived in a hut in a wood, studied Machiavelli and Marcus Aurelius, and took exercise on horseback with lance and ring.

News came- that the Turks were ravaging Hungary, and he equipped himself to join the Christian Army. But he was robbed on the way by four adventurers in France. Diverted for a time, he joined a ship engaged in what would be called piracy in these more fastidious days, but which was known then as a mer- chant vessel. In one of those lapses from its true vocation, to which even respectable ships of that period were liable, a Venetian argosy was plundered. This lucky capture put him in comfortable circumstances and enabled him to carry out his pious purpose of joining the Christian Army that was fighting the Turks.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0248.txt | included=true | included =====
"CHRISTIAN" WARFARE.

Against the Turks he devised many " excellent stratagems," and did prodigies of valour. He shone especially in the single combats, which were the fashion of the day. He slew a "Lord Turbashaw," also " one Grualgo," as well as "Bonny Mulgro," who tried to avenge the death of the other two. Very many remarkable things happened in the war, which the reader will find recorded in John Smith's autobiography. His bounding prowess received a check by reason of an un- fortunate wound. After a general engagement he was left for dead on the field of battle, was made prisoner and sold as a slave in Constan- tinople. It was his fortune or misfortune to be regarded with too much favour by his "fair mistresse," who " tooke much compassion on him." He was sent into the Crimea, where he was "no more regarded than a beast." Stirred to madness by the terrible oppression, he killed his taskmaster, the Ty-mor, put on his clothes, and mounting his horse rode across the Steppes, reaching, after many adventures, the Christian , land we call Germany.

ANOTHER CONTINENT SAMPLED. Most folks would have found their thirst for adventure satisfied—at least for a time. John Smith's own comment was that "being satisfied with Europe and Asia," and hearing of the " warres in Barbarie," he travelled into the in- terior of Morocco to sample another oontinent, and to find it full of materials for adventure. Satisfied for the time with achievements upon land, he "tried some conclusions at sea with the Spaniards." After a sea fight with two Spanish men-of-war he returned to England in 1604, having received 1,000 ducats as his share of the booty.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
AND YET ANOTHER.

- - - - —••• There remained a fourth continent to be sampled, and hopeful reports were coming to England of the New World across the Atlantic. It is not to be supposed that a man of John Smith's temperament could allow a new and promising continent to go unexplored, and in 1605 he joined the expedition of a London com- pany formed to colonise Virginia. The outward voyage was marked by some ugly incidents, in which Smith was accused of con- spiracy and narrowly escaped hanging. Things were anything but bright when he arrived. A great portion of the colonists had perished, and the survivors contemplated the abandonment of the enterprise. The qualities of the new- comer were soon recognised, and he found himself speedily at the .head of affairs--this very. 'much to the advantage of the colony, which began to improve under his energy and courage.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
A FAMOUS TRIP.

Responsibility ought perhaps to have sobered the intrepid Englishman and made him content to settle and toil in the midst of his fellow.. colonists. But nothing could cure the love of adventure.

Undermining Authority.

THE EFFECT OF THE ULSTER

OBJECT LESSON.

Mr. J. H. Thomas, M.P., addressing a large open-air meeting of railway workers at Darling- ton on Sunday, said that representatives of the National Union of Railwaymen and representa- tives of the railway companies had met in con- ference, and he was pleased to say that their negotiations had been conducted in a spirit con- ductive to, peace. They had conducted their de- liberations free, unfettered and untrammelled. At the same time it would be unfair, as it would not be fitting the truth, if he did not say that, in his judgment, there were syniptoms in the political position to-day that had a very im- por..ant hearing upon the situations. Whilst the leaders of the 300,000 railwaymen were ad- vocating constitutional methods in settling differences with employers, they were met on all hands with the cry " Why ,cannot we do, and why cannot you advise us to do, what other people are adviSing their men to do—arm them- selves to prepare for the worst?" (Cheers.) That was an aspect of the question to which they could not blind themselves. It rendered the posi- tion of the men's leaders both difficult and dan- gerous. Without committing himself, or giving away anything that had, taken place in the course of the negotiations, he would point out that if the rich meii. who defied law and order could be

r opinions of his own about these meddling white men whose own country was not big enough for them, and proceeded to deal with Captain John !Smith without the formality of a trial. He was tied to a tree, and the expert archersovere about to show their skill upon him when his quick brain hit on the idea of showing them his pocket compass. This kept their attention and gave him breathing and thinking time. Next came the idea of initiating them into the use of gunpowder. They were delighted at the knowledge, and carefully put away a bag of the new substance until the next spring "to plant as they did their come," says the inimit- able teller of the story, "because they would be acquainted with the nature of that seede." But gunpowder, weapons, compass only gave a temporary respite. King Powhatan had tile white man before him a second time, and orclered his instant execution. "Two great stones were brought; as many as could layd hands on him, dragged him to them, and there- on laid his bead, being ready with their clubs to beate out his braines." ENTER POCAHONTAS.

It is here that the graceful -1.-)o-CL-1-1;-ontas float into the story. The "King's dearest daughlmr," a bea Plucky, she had formed an opinion girl, nof the 'n' dWhite kin" that differed from that of her father. Re was worth keep- ing alive at any rate, and she used every entreaty. When entreaty failed she took des- perate measures. While his head rested upon the stones she rushed forward, "got his head in her armes, and laid her owne upon his to save him from death." The thick-skinned Pow- hatan gave in at this, and the adventurer was spared to add a few more chapterS+ to his uncommon career. Yet again the fair Poca- hontas saved his life by revealing a plot formed by her angry father, and eventually Smith got away. In 1613 Captain Argall swooped down upon the Indian tribe and carried away Pocahontas to Jamestown. - She seems to have taken kindly to white men and settled down among them. Never was captive more entirely satisfied with captivity. PET OF THE COLONY. The white men took to her with equal kind- ness. There was difficulty in the pronouncing of her name, but this was not insuperable. When she became converted to Christianity, they seized the opportunity to christen her Rebecca —a name much easier to spell when they wrote letters home to tell their friends about her. A year later, in 1614, she showed her further appreciation of white men by agreeing to marry John Rolfe, a settler who has another claim to fame as the first planter of tobacco in Virginia. She had children, and there are families in America to-day who boast of their descent from the dark-skinned daughter of King Pow- hatan.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
THE VISIT TO ENGLAND.

John Rolfe was evidently proud of his wife. That she was interesting need not be said, and it is equally unnecessary to say that the young colony made quite a fuss of her. In 1616 Rolfe brought her to England—and to fame. The Queen, with an eye for novelties, made a point of seeing her. She be- came the idol of thoe Court; common folks crowded to watch her in the streets. The Queen took her in state to see Ben Jon son's "Twelfth Night Masque," and she was; feted and feasted. It was to Captain John Smith, more than to any- one else that she owed her reception, for this delightful gentleman, as happy with his pen (and as brilliant) as with his sword, had pub- lished a glowing account of his adventures in the New World.

Pocahontas had a wonderful-seven months in England, and in March, 1617, had embarked, very reluctantly, to travel back to Virginia. But English hospitality had been too much for her; she died off Gravesend, and her body was brought ashore and buried in Gravesend Church. Born in 1595, she had hardly reached her 23rd birthday when death 'snatched her away.

This was the brave young woman whom the American .Ambassador and a group of notables assembled to commemorate the other day, and a memorial window will keep her story fresh. JOHN SMITH, AUTHOR.

Of Captain John Smith it would be pleasant to write more. This man of unromantic name; made romance wherever he went and spent his last years in writing his life-story. He was appointed President df the Colony of Virginia in 1608, but after being disabled by an explo- sion of gunpowder returned to England in 1609. From 1610 to 1617 he was again in Virginia, exploring panada and N. Virginia, which he named New England, interesting himself in fisheries and fur markets, and making things lively for Indian and settler alike. The last fourteen years of his life were spent in. London. That there might be no erroneous ideas concerning his manifold adventures, he busied himself to write two detailed treatises : "The True Travels, Adventures, and Observations of Captain John Smith in Europe, Asia, Africke, and America," an'd "The Generall Historie of Virginia, New England and the Somner Isles." These entertaining books were published in 1627 and 1629.

AN UNEXPECTED END. John Smith adopted as his crest a chevron and three Turks' heads, commemorating the three dusky heads he had cut off in Hungary. He died on June 21st, 1631. The unexpected sometimes happened even to Elizabethan adventurers and pirates. He died in bed.

(Cheers.) In other words, the position was this. If our troops were to be allowed to say, "My conscience will not allow me to do a certain thing in Ulster," then the working class conscience must be aroused, and they must be permitted to say, "Troops shall not interfere in any industrial dispute between capital and labour." (Cheers.) He was, however, opposed to such conduct by both classes. It was essen- tial that laws should be recognised and obeyed. He could conceive of nothing that had so tended to undermine authority as the incident he had referred to, and he, as a leader of men, would be false to every principle he held dear, and to the very class to which he belonged, if he did not here mid now say to these people, "You are sowing what you do not expect to reap. You are sowing seed and ideas in the minds of the working classes that will not be forgotten by them when the time arrives." He recognised that in the negotiations that were going on between the railway companies and the men, the companies were endeavouring to meet the situation. He had confidence in the spirit in which they were being conducted. He believed that, in spite of the difficultie§, they would be able to emerge from the negotiations with a scheme that would be satisfactory to the men, that would ensure that all little pin-pricks should be dealt with in a practical way, and lead to the removal of \ barriers that tended towards war and not to peace. As the railway companies had recognised trade unions, the latter wished to show that they were worthy of recognition;, and they desired to approach questions of difference in a broad and generous spirit, for they knew their cause was a just and• righteous one. (Cheers.)

Literary Gleanings.

FIRST VOYAGE OF THE AQUITANIA. The Aquitania, on her first voyage from Liver- pool to New York, did splendidly. It will be remembered that the aim of the design was to ensure a mean speed on the Atlantic of 23 knots within a year from the entrance of the vessel in 'service. Although the vessel had only been under steam for forty-eight hours, on her way from the Clyde to Liverpool, before she left the Mersey on her voyage to New York, she made the trip of 3,181 nautical miles in 5 days 17 lours 43 minutes, which gives an average speed throughout the whole voyage of 23.1 knots. It must be remembered, however, that the vessel slowed down for three hours to a very low speed owing to fog, which fact presaged the presence of ice in the vicinity. This is clearly brought out in the runs per day. For the first day out the average speed was 23.17 knots, on the second day 23.07 knots, on the third day 24.24 knots; while on the next day, when the fog was ex- perienced, the average speed was reduced to 21.34 knots by the stoppage; on the last complete day of the voyage the average was 24.26 knots. On the two days when the weather conditions admitted of the full speed being run the dis- tance covered frnm noon to noon was 602 miles. There is thus every promise that the vessel will attain an average of 24 knots throughout the whole Atlantic voyage. A feature noted by the passengers was the absence of vibration. The Aquitania, notwithstanding her great size, was berthed in New York Harbour within 20 min- utes of her arrival.—" Engineering."

===== 0002601_19140725_art0255.txt | included=true | included =====
EUROPEAN ARMAMENT.

An Italian specialist has invented an apparatus by means of which he is said to be able to set fire to powder magazines, torpedoes, and other explosives at a distance of nineteen kilometers unerringly. No medium—be it metal, stone, earth or water—can shut out the projected rays that burn. They seek and find their object, automatically, unfailingly. The principle is scientific, and the ,mechanism, which is simple, has been several times tested during the past few weeks and found efficient. As yet it is prem- ature to speculate on the results which may ultimately be achieved by this marvellous in- vention, on the far-reaching changes which it may bring about in warfare, or on its eventual influence on the worst evils of militarism. But even at this stage one cannot but perceive The vast potentialities of Signor Ulivi's discovery and invention.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0256.txt | included=true | included =====
ALBANIA.

Albania is thus become the powder magazine of the Balkans, whereas it possesses all the mak- ings of a little State more prosperous, better ordered, and more efficiently defended than Montenegro. The country is fertile. Large tracts of forest land contain the best timber to be found in Europe—secular trees which can- not be matched between the Balkan peninsula and California. Various minerals, excellent tobacco, olives, vines, and in two districts naphtha, give promise of future economic well- being. But they call for systematic exploita- tion, which is still impossible. The people, too, are extremely docile, and, when skilfully taken in hand, faithful and devoted. Turkish mis- rule, endured for ages, has left its mark on their habits and ways of thought.

===== 0002601_19140725_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDHOOD, TERROR, AND THE GROTESQUE.

It is vain to regret- this —passing of..th* Gro- tesques. They went with the elves and goblins, with Puck and all his Triends. No art can bring them back. If a craftsman set himself eow to carve the animals, the 4ueer faces, the gar- goyles, the mopping and mowing crew that entered into possession of the Gothic cathedrals, he would do it self-consciously, following after his forefathers. Ile would work with a smile. But the mediseval carver worked, one knows it, with the portentous gravity of a child. To the modern a pious joke is unseemly. It was not SO in the Middle Ages. A religious jest was wholly proper. Genial malice was always at home in church. Moreover, in the safety of that sanctuary you might jeer at the devil himself. He and his grinning abominable dependents were your laughing stock. You could afford to make fun of them when the cathedral roof rose above your head. The child is a born artist, but he lacks ex- pression and technical skill. By the time that he can acquire these his golden store of fancy has vanished. It is the leprechaun's gold. Some elect souls keep the memory of what they would have said till the time when they can say it. They learn expression in some school; but what theyl would express they learnt from Nature in the days before their thoughts formed any sequence, or could be called thoughts. For the most vivid things in a child's mind are not thcuights, they are impressions. There is noth- ing in all this world as sensitive to impression as a child's nature. Long, long before he has any names for what lie knows, his mind is stored with pictures of things seen. What is essential to him his imagination will hold.—Mrs. W. M. Letts in the "Contemporary Review" for July.

ST. HELENS SCENES.

Details of a seties of week-end assaults on St. Helens police were given at the local police court on Monday. Constable Hurst said that shortly after midnight on Saturday he found n, man named Thomas Fitzpatrick causing a great dis- turbance in Union-street. Prisoner suddenly took a leap at him and knocked him down. Prisoner fought like a madman, and called on the crowd to help him. They were met by a shower of bricks and stones, and another con- stable was struck on the temple by a brick which felled him to the ground, where prisoner began kicking him. William Williams, a vagrant, was fined 20s. and costs or a month for butting a constalie in the stomach when he arrested him. _ _

Thomas Bolan, a young man who had been locked up along with a companion for gambling, was charged with assaulting Constable Heyes. Bolan was so violent that another constable had to release two other prisoners to go and assist Reyes. Bolan was fined ss. and costs for gambling, and 20s. and costs for the assault.

A LONG WORD. Transubstantiationslists and antidisestablish- mentaranism are long words, but their 24 and 28 letters are dwarfed by the names of a new form of cheese, which is described in " L'lllus- tration." The cheese is made of fresh curdled milk, flavoured with the dry, finely-cut blossoms of Asperula odorata, or woodruff, a European herb. The originality of this new dairy product, however, rests largely in the name under which it has been put' on the market. Oberammer- gau erpa ssions fes tSp iel al p en kra uterklo sterdeli- catesfrustuckskase. It has 73 letters and occu- pies two full lines of print in the " Butter and Egg Merchants' Gazette." When translated it signifies—Cheese of the cloister, a breakfast deli- cacy flavoured with herbs of the Alps, prepared for the feast of the representation of the Pas- sion at Oberammergau. " Without doubt," says " L'lllustration," " if the cheese had been bap- tised in French it would have received a more concise name." Probably in England or America something shorter would be made to serve.

THE DEEP SEA FISHERS.

The good ship "Sofa" heaves and dips amid the

- smaller sada And here, with string and crooked pin, we sit

and fish for whales._

All day we fish with might and main the Par-

lour Carpet Sea, For oh, the larder's empty, and we must get

food for tea! What though about our slender craft the billows

beat and roar? What though great, greedy, green-eyed sharks infest the parlour floor? Our relatives depend on us, and tea-time's draw-

ing nigh, If food they lacked, we could not bear to see them sit and cry. Then, hungry uncles, starving aunts, and fam- ished parents dear, Who wait us anxiously on shore, we'll feed you, never fear. What ho, a bite—a whale at last! Our tiny ship careens! The household's saved! There's food for all— a tin of fine sardines!

ONLY A DOG.

Commonly called a man's most faithful friend, brought home a mere handful in the master'b pocket, tearing and biting every loose article, paying little or no heed to quiet scolding, patted one moment, scolded the next, yet still continu- ing its career of small destructions.

Gradually the dumb animal begins to know its master's footsteps, waiting on the door-mat to give welcome and receive a quiet word and gentle pat. It may be true that he possesses no soul; he certainly is unable to express him- self in words, yet he gives unmistakable signs in impressive language, though - his truthful eyes, and his equally expressive tail of confi- dence and affection. Only a dumb animal! Ak, well! think one moment of the lesson it teaches, a lesson—nay, more than a lesson. That little doggie, speaking in language of its own with faithfulness and loving glances is true as steel, showing innate "true feeling and intelligence.

A light left burning, a -little dog's barking, have saved many homes. The burglar does not care for the "little bark "; it gives a warning, so he passes on his way. More than once the writer has come down in the small hours, hearing the warning bark. "Well, doggie! What is all this noise about?" Sheepish the little barker looks, nay, as.if ashamed, but there was no cause for shan*. More than once I have found on investigation that a burglar had made a hurried retreat over the yard door, leaving "torn bits" of• his trousers behind him—clear evidence that the little ' watcher had made burglary for that night impossible. Experience often shows the doggie feels that his master is forgetting the morning walk, and he will come quietly and lick one's hands, looking up with wistful eyes. " Well, doggie! What is it? Do you want to go out?" Doggie may be dumb, but he speaks joyfully enough, rushing off to the door barking with pleasure, then looking up and seeming almost to ask "Which way are we going?" What matter that your walk may have been somewhat lengthy? Doggie only shows the more satisfaction on returning home, sighing deeply as he throws his little body in graceful abandon at his master's feet. No wonder the owner feels grief when the dumb friend is called away. Never say that your faithful little pet has lived and died for nothing, leaving a memory only behind. I envy no one who can accept the loss with indifference and sneer at your exhibition of feel- ings of sorrow in losing such an attached com- panion.

"GOING TO STICK IT."

Mr. J. F. L. Brunner and Public _Business.

-•- Speaking at Northwich on Saturday, Mr. J. F. L. Brunner, M.P.,. said they were beginning to have their hard time in the House of Com- mons. The suspension of the eleven o'clock rule had been moved, and they Would have to sit up until the Minister in charge was too tired to go on. The Liberal party, however, were going to stick it. They were not going to let the Tories snap them, even though-they were going to be in readiness outside of London for any signal.

RAILWAY ACCIDENT AT CARLISLE.

A North British express from Edinburgh was waiting outside Carlisle Station about two o'clock on Sunday morning, when it was run into in the rear by a Caledonian express from Glasgow, which was fortunately not travelling at a high speed. About a dozen passengers-re- ceived minor injuries, and two cases were treated .at fhe infirmary, but all were able to proceed to their destination.

THE CARBON PROCESS.

It is difficult to understand the apparent un- popularity of this process, for it has many ad- vantages that other printing methods do not possess. For example, the worker has almost an unlimited range of colours to choose from. He can prepare his print on almost any material he can think of, paper, card, glass, ivory, wood, metal, canvas, and numerous other substances being available. He can produce a rich quality of effect unattainable with many more popular processes, and he can rely on the permanence of the results. Further than this, there is a peculiar charm about the working of the pro- cess, for to our minds the most ra-scinating operation in photography is watching the ap- pearance of the carbon image "in the hot water used for development. In comparison, the de- velopment of a bromide print is a dull operation, while that of a platinum print is too rapid and mechanical to arouse much interest. Possibly one cause of the neglect of carbon is the mis- taken idea that special plant is required, but the only special plant is a large dish that can be kept hot, and this may be a cheap zinc affair put together by a local ivhitesmith, or a deep enamelled steel dish, or even a porcelain one. We have frequently used the last-mentioned type of dish, simply standing it on a sheet of asbestos board resting on a tripod to prevent the flame from the burner underneath from actually touching the dish. One of the advantages of carbon is that nothing but water is required for development, while there is no fixing process. No chemical troubles can therefore exist which may result in spoiling the print. There are no chemicals to wash out and the actual process of developing and finishing is soon completed.— "British Journal of Photography."

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY. Excursion by Cycle to Kelsoll.—The pleasure value of a combined cycling and photographic outing was put to a severe test on Saturday, and the eight members who joined deserve the thanks of the society for keeping to the printed fixture, in spite of a blazing sun and flies with- out number. They set off by the most direct road through Acton Bridge, Crowton and Nor- ley, but instead of carrying out the leader's pro- gramme and taking tea at Eddisbury, they were compelled to stop at Hatchmere, and the meal was served at Hardman's. After a long rest in the grounds two returned home on their motor and silecar, whilst .the others enjoyed an even- ing spin through the Forest, two eventually reaching Kelsoll. In spite of these successive sheddings, however, many good snapshots and , views were obtained, especialb, at the "Look- out " on Birch Hill, from whence a charming panorama is obtained of the Chester plain and the Welsh hill country.

PHOTOGRAPHIC HOLIDAYS. The Peovers.—There are three Peovers—Lower and tipper, and Nether, which also means "lower," and are all within four miles south of Knutsford. These four miles abound with sub- jects for the camera—beautiful cottages, creeper or rose covered houses, several fine old inns, and the distant views of Dutch town.

Higher Peover Church is in the park attached to the hall, and not often visited by photo- graphers. But that at Lower Peover amply makes up for it. There happens to be an excel- lent picture of it in last week's "Amateur Photography," also the very best view obtain- able, and it is there called "a beautiful example of black and white ecclesiastical architecture." It dates from the 13th century, and the wood- work inside is as quaint as its outward appear- ance. Several fine oak screens adorn the church, within one of which is the old deed chest, lid and box being made of a single block from a tree. In the corner of the choir is a chained Bible. To get into the pews on the righb-hand side of the church a wooden barrier more thag a foot high must be stepped over; these were the labourers' pews, and were filled with rushes in the winter, to act as foot-warmers. The barrier, of course, prevented the rush or straw from being kicked over the church. The parish room, the school-house, and several neighbouring houses are well worth a plate, whilst other pic- tures may be found doWn at the brookside and the mill. The new car from Latchford to Knuts- ford ought to open up this fine country to War- rington holiday makers. The best house of rest, other than the inns, is Hoyle's; Pinewood Cot- tage, Middlewich-road.

WOMAN'S WORLD.

of importance, as far as women were concerned, gave way to the burning desire to make the best use of these shopping opportunities. Towards the end of the sales the chances for securing bargains are often greater than at the begin- ning. Ribbons are to be much used. Blouses, even dresses, are made of them, and sashes of ribbon are much more satisfactory than piece silk. Many beautiful pieces may be secured to- wards the close of the sales for a mere song, and these can be used for waistcoats, scarves, and, of course, _hat trimmings. Not only are ribbons highly glazed, but materials also. One of the latter bears a close resemblance to chintz in its appearance and pattern, but it is soft and pliable as silk. In combination with plain linen this fabric is effectively used for revers, cuffs, waistcoats and other accessories.

The Revival of the Fur Tipget. Furs in July sound out of place, but it is just now that bargains in fur are to be picked up, and the wise woman always looks ahead when matters sartorial are concerned. A glimpse of the peltry designed for wear in the autumn— which will come all too soon—reveals the fact that mole will take a prominent place. a's it de- serves to do. The fur tippet, modelled on the 1830 design, has been revived, but in modified forms, to suit present-day styles of dress. One model is rather a scarf than a tippet. The fur arranged in strips formed diagonal lines in front, with a V-shaped opening. At the back the effect was similar to a scarf. There were no ends, the strips of fur meeting and finishing at the waist. This is a very pretty little gar- ment in moleskin, and a purchase at sale price should turn out well.

The Fur Cape. Another model was something like the old- fashioned pelerine. It had very wide stole ends in front, and finished in a point at the back, just below the waist. This point is a feature of fur capes for the winter, and is seen not only on the tippet, but on the cloak. The cape or cloak that has carried all before it this spring and summer will be shown in a multitude of designs in cloth and satin, but more especially in fur, as soon as the sales are well over, and autumn goods can be displayed. Some of the new models in coney seal and caracul are par- ticularly pleasing and graceful. HINTS ON HEALTH. The Question of Universal Epidemics.

Mr. Roger W. Babson says that, in looking up appendicitis cases, he learned that in 17 per cent. of the operations for that disease the post- mortem examinations showed that the appendix was in perfect condition. "The whole subject," he adds, "reminds me of a true story I heard in London recently. In the hospitals there, the ailment of the patient, when he is admitted, is denoted by certain letters, such as T. B.' for tuberculosis. An American doctor was examin- ing these history slips, when his curiosity was aroused by the number on which the letters G. 0. K.' appeared. Ile said to the physician who was showing him round: "There seems to be a severe epidemic of this G. 0. K. in Lon- don. What is it, anyhow?" Oh, that means "God only knows,"' replied the English phy- sician."—" Open Door."

D isi nf ect ion .—The public health authority will always, when requested, disinfect rooms and any things requiring dry heat, such as mat- tresses, blankets, cloth clothes, or other woollen garments. As these articles must be taken away, wrap them in a sheet wetted with carbolic solu- tion (1 in 40) in readiness to be called for. All crockery should be washed and stood in carbolic lotion before being allowed to pass out of the room. Underclothes, bed-linen, &c., should also be well soaked in carbolic before being sent to the laundry. The laundry authorities should be notified of the nature of the fever, as the clothes will have to be kept separate from the ordinary washing and be well boiled in water containing plenty of soda.

By MARJORIE.

A Woman Doctor of Laws. 'Manchester University has this year conferred I the honorary degree of LL.D on one of Old- ham's most distinguished citizens, Mrs. S. A. Lees, who is a member of the Court of Man- chester University, and was among its first graduates This lady, who was Mayor of Old- ham, was presented with the freedom of the borough and made the returning officer at the, last election. She could not, however, sit on, the magistrates' bench, although the mayor of the town. Mrs. Lees attended the Coronation in the latter capacity, and her dress for that occasion caused the Duke of Norfolk some little anxiety.

A Versatile Englishwoman. Women's names do not figure in the birthday honours, but some are to be found on the Civil Pension list, and among these is that of Mrs. Traquaire, whose versatility is nothing short of amazing. Not only did this lady assist her late husband in I is scientific work, but she under- took the dec•-,ration of the walls of the Catholic and Apostoi_c Cathedral of Edinburgh, land the choir school of St. Mary's Cathedral in the same city, as well as the Royal Hospital for sick children. She is a skilled embroideress, and exhibited her work in the Paris Exhibition of 1900. She took up book-binding, by accident, and is a prominent member of the Women's Bookbinders' Guild. She has mastered also the art of enamelling, in which she excels, as in everything else she undertakes.

This Season's Sales. The summer sales are every year on a more extensive scale, and prices are generally lower and the bargains more tempting. One London 'store made provision for the reception of a mil- lion customers during one week, but this num- ber was considerably exceeded, if the orders that came by post and telephone were taken into account. IHEAIS. FOR MOTHERS.

The time to begin caring for the hair is in childhood. If the small girl's hailt is well cared for the grown woman reaps the benefit. But there is no period when the hair cah be safely neglected; it must receive its full meed of attention when it is white as much as when it was brown or golden. It is not everyone who can have long and abundant tresses any more than everyone can have a pink and white complexion. Nature has something to say in the matter. But every- one can have lustrous, healthy hair that sug- gests what, for want of a better term, is called good "grooming." The Hair and the Health. It is not generally understood that the condition of the hair is dependent to a great extent on the general state of health. The hair de- pends for its nourishment on the blood, just as does the skin, and when one is out of health or is worried or overworked the lowered vitality will tell on the hair almost as soon as it does on the complexion. So when the hair falls out badly, or is harsh and lustreless, it is advisable to look to the general physical condition first of all.

Mumps.—The symptoms are, at first, a feeling

_ of being out of sorts, generally. Then after a few days the real trouble makes itself felt, by _ . .

feverishness, pain and swelling behind or below the jaw, made worse by speaking or swallowing. First one side is affected, and then the other.

THE STAMP WATCHER'S FAITH.

JOSEPH HOCKING (9.nthor of " Miatress Nancy Molesworth.." "The Wilderness," etc.).

The story I have to tell .has come to mT knowledge during the last few days. It le only a little more than a week ago since I came to my native county—surely the most beautiful in all England,—and yet the events I have to relate have taken place within that time. The cynics say that ro- mance, if it ever existed, has been dead for ages; but ;,he cynics are blind. There is a beautiful world that they never see. There is music of which they know nothing. They only hear the ravens croaking never- more," while all the time the heavens are full of the singing, birds of God. It was last Tuesday I remember that I went out for a walk alone. An editor of a magazine had asked me to write a story for him; and I had come to the sunny south in order to write it amidst congenial surround- ings. "Strike the note of romance," he had said, "let our readers feel that things are in the spring time, and that the day is at the morn." And so I had come out alone, to dream if I could, and to see visions of the land of love and beauty. I had reached a lonely dell, and sat down in a sheltered nook. Behind me, just over the hill was a glorious view of the rock bound coast of Cornwall. I had been watching the waves rolling upon the giant headlands, and list- ening to the scream of the sea birds as they floated over the shining sea. But here in the valley all was silent save for the thud of the "stamps " a little distance away. The noise was not musical; but it was pleas- ant to me. It reminded me of my boyhood days when I watched the giant hanuners r i nd the tin ore into powder. As, I listened I was a boy a-ain, the intervening years were as nothilg; life was young, and all the world was before me. " Mornin', tut!, I looked up and saw a crabbed-looking old man; he was leaning on a stick, and was watching me curiously. I could see that he was eager to talk with me. "Good morning," I replied. " Not that there's much good 'bout it," he added presently. Not much good ~ I queried. "What can you. want more ? March is nearly at an end, the primroses are thick oa the hedges, the birds are singing, the Run is ehinin,„ and the air is as sweet as the nectar of tlie gods." WOMEN WHO HAVE

PASSED AS MEN.

The motives which have induced some wo- men to renounce their sex, and pass at least part of their lives in the guise of men are as various as tire impulses of human nature it- self, whilst the success which has generally rewarded their efforts might be taken as some slight argument in favour of the mod- ern demand for equality. A BRAVE WOMAN-SOLDIER. It was with the object of finding out the whereabouts of her runaway husband, a Dutch sailor, that Hannah Snell, the daugh- ter of a Worcester hosier, first put on male attire. Having no means of travelling at her own expense, she thought the best way of attaining her ()bled would be by joining the army, and in tee November of 1745 she entered the corps of General Guise at Coven- try. She was almost immediately transferred to Carlisle, and here, at the very outeet of her military career, her troubles began. Her loyalty to her own sex induced her to inter- fere in the love affairs of a. too gay Lothario in the uniform of a sergeant; and because of this, and not _for a trumped up charge of dereliction of duty, she was sentenced to re- ceive six hundred lashes, at the Carlisle Gate. She bore the punishment bravely, and at the same time managed to keep up the secret of her sex. As may be supposed, this experi- ence did not tend to make the service more agreeable in her eyes, but she might still have continued her career as a soldier were it not for the advent of a new recruit who had known her as a woman. Fear of discov- , ery now induced her to desert, and she is next heard of as a marine on board the sloop of 'war "Swallow" at Portsmouth, destined to form part of Boscawen's fleet in the East Indies. In the attack on Mauritino, Hannah made herself conspicuous by reckless bravery, as she did a little later on at the siege of lArez•copong. So much was this the case, that she was chosen as one of a small band of the most valiant men of the fleet, got together for the purpose of bringing stores from the shore. This work was successfully carried through], and Hannah again distinguished herself by avenging a comrade's death, kil- ling his murderer by her own hands. ON GUARD DURING A SIEGE. At the siege of Pondicherry she suffered the most severe hardships, being on guard seven nights in suocesslon, and half the time up to her shoulders in water. As for wounds, she came out with twelve. After leaving the hospital she was put on board the man-o'- war "Eltham," and here she was once more unjustly condemned to bear the pain and agony of the lash. She was accused of steal- ing a shirt, but her real offence was that she had refused to sing at the dictation of a dom- ineering lieutenant, in whose possession the shirt was afterwards found. But James Gray, as she was called, had already borne his twelve lashes. On the way home, she learned that the husband, for love of whom she had borne so much, had perished at the hands of the public executioner. She had no longer any motive for continuing her dis- guise, and as soon as she had received her pay she went back to petticoats. Soon after she appeared as a "draw" at the theatre at Goodman's Fields, though the exhibition of herself was little to her taste. A pension of .230 a year, however, obtained for her by the Duke of York, enabled her to live in retire- ment the rest of her life. A NOBLE PURPOSE. On a much higher social scale was another woman-soldier, Frances Scanaaetti of Milan who assumed man's attire for the noble pur- pose of covering up the cowardice of a be- loved brother. On the way to join the mili- tary academy at Neustadt, this brother, more from fear of a soldier's life than from a purely physical ailment, took ill. Frances persuaded him to return home, and then tak- ing possession of his papers and assutaing his clothes and name, she journeyed on to the college alone. At the time she was only thirteen years of age, anti at the end of an- other two years she had not only, carried off the principal prizes in the intellectual field, but had become a most expert fencer, and a proficient in military tactics. In addition to her native Italian tongue, she knew the English, German and French languages, and altogether her character stood so high that she could not be refused the commission she applied for in the Austrian Military Council. As ensign in the regiment of St. George, she campaigned on the Rhine for six- teen months, till the peace of Campo Formio sent her to the town of Sandomir. Here her sex began to be suspected by the ladies, but by an extra gallantry of manner she man- aged to still keep up the delusion. In 1799 she was removed to Poland, where she met the French in various encounters, on one oc- casion being the first to enter the enemy's re- doubts, sword in hand. At Leghorn, soon af- ter the conclusion of the Polish campaign, Frances saw her parents. for the first ti7-ke "eaft Nam Nowak WINVOIe. Nam Noon Wok Nam. Nook Wok wl" tem vim wick Nom, VOW lOW

Hats and Buttons. The fabric hat is having a vogue, particularly in white satin, simply trimmed with black or coloured velvet pansies. In black velvet, trimmed with white wings, or in white moire, the brim lined with a colour, it is equally favoured, and quite suited to summer wear. No one need be unbecomingly hatted this year, for no one style predominates, and there are shapes to suit all faces. There are sevTral new ways of using ostrich feathers on hats, of which that of placing this decoration flatly on the brim, just as if it were about to be pressed in an album, is novel, but scarcely artistic. A mount consist- ing of a number of different feathers is a novelty, and in some instances forms the sole trimming. Buttons are an important item of waistcoats and other garments, and jet is used again very freely. A new design looks like a glass marble dropped into an enamelled frame.

The Old-fashionet: Sun Bonnet. , There has been quite a demand this summer for the old "Early Victorian" sun bonnets. Thanks very largely to the efforts of a lady resi- dent, Mrs. Jocelyn Pickard-Cambridge, the making of this old-world headgear has been re- vived as a cottage industry in the village of Bloxworth, near Wareham, Dorsetshire. The , material used for these bonnets is the real old- fashioned print or zephyr, and the Bloxworth shape, which is cane stiffened to form the " poke," is most becoming. everal of these bonnets, which cost about 4s. each, were on view at the recent Arts and Industries Exhibi- tion in London, when Queen Alexandra bought some, and expressed herself very pleased with their make and design.

The Black Page. Time was when fashionable ladies were at- tended when they paid calls, or took their walks abroad, by tiny black pages dressed in Moorish costume. The wife of a celebrated artist has revived the custom. When she returned from Tangier she was accompanied by a page in native dress, who is her special attendant, and very devoted to his mistress.

COOKERY CORNER. Pear Chips—A Pleasant Dish.

This is an excellent recipe that you really must try when the time comes. It is, I admit, a little early. Wipe eight pounds of pears, re- T:love the stems and skins and cores, divide into quarters; some people even divide into quite mall pieces or chips. Add four pounds of sugar and four ounces of Canton ginger, let it stand over-night. In the morning add four lemons cut into small pieces, rejecting the seed; cook slowly for three hours. Put into stone jars.

Preserved Melon Rind.—This is another rather uncommon preserve, but you like uncommon things, do you not This has the great virtue also of being very economidal ! Save your melon rinds, madam, and convert them into a preserve. Pare and cut them into strips, being careful to throw away any over-ripe portions. Soak them in alum water to cover them, allowing two tea- spoonfuls of powdered alum to each quart of water. Heat gradually to boiling point and cook slowly for ten minutes. Drain, cover with ice water, if possible, and let it stand two hours. Again drain and dry between towels. Weigh it and allow one pound of sugar to each pound of fruif, and one breakfast cupful of water. Boil the sugar and water for ten minutes, add the preserved melon rind and cook until quite tender. Remove the rind to a stone jar and cover with syrup. Two lemons, cut in slices, may be cooked ten minutes in the syrup.

Cheese Omelette is a most delicious and savoury dish, and quickly and easily made.

A ROMANTIC CAREER.

It was love of a different kind, but at least equally romantic, which induced Mary East, belonging to one of the eastern counties, to pass most of her life as a man, under the name of James How. At the age of sixteen she was engaged to a young fellow who, to say the least, must have been unworthy of her. Convicted of robbery, he was sentenced to transportation for life. Mary was incon- solable, and to mark her grief, she resolved, for the sake of her lover, to remain single all her life. Meeting another girl, whose case was somewhat similar to her own, the two re- solved to live together, one of them, for bet- ter protection, to assume the attire of a man. The masculine part fell to Mary, al- though she was a year younger than her com- panion. They had thirty pounds between them, and with this sum they managed to es- tablish themselves at a small inn at Epping in Essex, under the names of Mr. and Mrs. How. Here they lived until an action at law brought by " Mr. How" against a young gentleman with whom he had quarrelled re- sulted in a verdict of £5llO. With this sum the happy couple removed to larger premises at Limehouse-Hole and soon began to save money. After a time they were enabled to take a still more respectable house, the White House at Poplar, and throve so well that at the end of twenty years from the be- ginning of the strange partnership they were the joint owners of considerable property. But the advent of a blackmailer, a woman who had known Mary East in her youth, and now demanded payment for her silence, greatly interfered with their happiness. They continued to thrive, however, and more than once James How figured creditably in some public capacity, such as foreman of a jury, guardian of the poor, etc. Not until the death of the "wife" was the well-kept secret revealed. "Mrs. How" revealed the whole ciroumstanoss to Iror brother, but Mary did not resume the proper garb of her sex until she appeared as a witness against the woman who had blackmailed her, and then her petticoats gave her so much trouble that she kept the court in continuo= laugh. ter.

A FAMOUS AUTHOR'S DISGW,SE. A Character, far more famous than any of the foregoing, who for a period put on the kind of dress she was not born to, was Susan- na Centlivre, author of "A Bold Stroke for a Wife," and about a score of other plays. As so often happens in fiction, it was a cruet Ettepmother that drove Susanna Freeman from her home in Ireland, when she was but fifteen years of age. By means which are not known she got across to England, with little or no money in her pocket; and started out to walk to London. With good spirits she kept on for a time and then, overcome by fa- tigue, she sat down by the wayside and wept. And as in the sonz the "Snuire's Soti" came tiding hy and took pity on her. His name was Anthony Hammond, destined to become known afterwards as a literary man, and he offered to take her to live with him in his rooms at Cambridge University. When they reached their destination she was attired in a boy's suit, and as Master Hammond's "cous- in Jack" she lived the ordinary life of the University for a time, reading and studying with a good deal of industry. When the two, through fear of discovery by the authorities, agreed to part, it was with the intention of getting married later on. But the promise was broken on one side or the other. Susan- na however did not come short of a husband. She married in the first instance Mr. Fox, a nephew of Sir Charles Fox, and afterwards Mr. Joseph Centlivre, the liina'a bead c.f.*. VIM

" It's just a dream of an old English home," and then somehow I know not why, but I thought of Paul Pentenwin, and pictured him as night after night he sat alone attending to the stamps that pounded the tin ore to powder. I went to see him that night as I had de- termined, but scarcely a word would he spe.q.k to me, although he almost eagerly as- sented to my suggestion that I should come again. On Wednesday night I found him otill uncorn,municative, although I thought he looked at me with a more friendly eye. On Friday I again made my way over the hill towards the little mine where Paul Pen- tenwin spent his nights. It was a glorious night; the wind which had been rather bois- terous through the day had sunk to rest, and the sky was cloudless. The moon, too, sailed serene, and majestic overhead making the whole scene like fairyland. When came to the stamps, the place looked desert- ed The water splashed over the water wheel while the stamps fell with a regular and monotonous thud on the hard stones, but beyond this I could see nor hear nothing. Presently, however, I saw the watchman's hut, mud within it a light was burning dim- ly. "I expect Paul Pentenwin is there," said to myself, and then I found my way to the little wooden house.

" Can I come in?' I asked, but no answer came to my question. A little later how. ever I heard a rustling noise, and saw Paul Pentenwiu rise from a kind of hutch where he'd been sitting.

He gave me an anxious scared kind of look, and then sighed deeply. "I hope you'll excuse me," I said, "but I thought I would drop in for another chat. ' You may remember that I said I should." "Yes, I remember," he replied. " I've heard your name since. Won't you sit down ?" and he pointed to a roughly made stool. I sat down, and in the light of the candle aided by the flames which leapt up the roughly made fire place I cOuld see his face. No it was not the face of a madman, and yet there was something strange abbot it. his eyes burned with a curious light too. " I've read some of your books," he said presently. "Yes ?" I queried. "Yes. You—you will understand." I was not quite sure what he meant, co I waited for him to proceed. " I suppose Eli Keast has told you all about me ? That is all he knows ?" "He spoke to me about you," I replied. "They 4:2.1 c 2.17 lam mad,' he said after pause. 'But I'm not. might have been at one time, for it was a terrible accident, but not now. It would be no wonder if I were, but I am not. No, I am not." I was silent for I saw that he was think- ing deeply, and it seemed to me as though it would be sacrilege to disturb him. "Of course my story is common proper- ty," he went on. "Everyone in this neigh- bourhood know the affairs of everyone else. I let them think what they will." "I think you are wise," I said. "The people, kind-hearted as they are; cannot un- derstand you. You are happier thinking your own thoughts, and peopling your world according to your own fancy." " Ah, yes, you see, you see. I say nothing to anyone. They don't understand—neither do they know for that matter." "Not know ?" I repeated. "No, they do not know. They think she is married; but she is not. She will come back to me." He was perfectly calm and collected, and yet I feared that the villagers were right. The poor young' fellow's Duna was withal.

TRINITY SCHOOL.

Rejection of Claim for Preferential

Treatment.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE FIRM.

At a meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday night, Councillor H. Roberts presiding, The School Management Sub-committee re- ported that correspondence with the managers of the Trinity. School, in which the managers state—on the 13th June, 1914—that they "re- quire to have an efficient experienced and well- qualified teacher to assist Miss Davies at once"; and further—on the 23rd June, 1914—that "the managers, in order to safeguard the trust-deed of the school, have been compelled to give Miss Bullen notice that her services as head mistress of the infants' department cannot be continued, and will terminate three months from this date," was submitted, and it was resolved (a) That the managers be informed that the committee cannot accede to their request for additional staff, because the school is already efficiently staffed in accordance with the consid- ered regulations of the committee as applied to all the public elementary schools in the borough; (b) That this committee do not consent to the action of the managers of the Trinity School in giving notice of dismissal to Miss Emily Bullen, the head mistress of the infants' department; (c) That the Director of Education be in- structed to communicate this latter decision to the head mistress of the infants' department, and to convey to her an expression of the com- mittee's continued confidence in her.

CANON STEVENSON'S VIEWS. Canon MORLEY STEVENSON, dealing with the minute, said the School Management Sub-com- mittee worked under regulations for staffing laid down by that committee, from which they never departed. In that case there had been no de- parture. Holy Trinity School had been treated in precisely the same way as any other school in the town would have been treated in similar circumstances. (Hear, hear.) Consequently, he was at a loss to understand the connection be- tween safeguarding the trustees and dispensing with the services of the head mistress of the infants' department. The sub-committee saw no reason why the head mistress should be dis- missed and no reason was given for the safe- guarding of the trustees. He could not see any connection between the two, and he was sor-Fy to see that the managers had seen fit to take such a step. (Hear, hear.) It was impossible for the sub-committee to agree to it, and he was sorry to see so unpleasant an incident brought forward. He moved the confirmation of the minute. Councillor OSBORNE seconded, and the minutes, as passed by the sub-committee, were approved.

CHESHIRE RIFLE ASSOCIATION.

The annuaT meeting or the Cheshire Rifle Association was held at Chester on Monday, under the chairmanship of Colonel W. Mother- sill, V.D. Colonel R. Thompson, secretary of the Cheshire Territorial Association, proposed and Lieutenant-Colonel E. W. Grey seconded, that the Marquis of Crewe be invited to become the new president of the Association. The an- nual report, which was adopted, showed a credit balance of £4 us. 6d. The retiring members of the Council were re-elected. The fifth prize meeting was fixed to be held at Altcar on Satur- day, August 8, when prizes to the value of over £3OO will be offered. During a discussion at- tention was called to a decision of the Council that the Extra Series Competition for this year shall be confined to those serving in the county or members of the Association. When a man who has had a great sorrow, lives a life apart from his fellow men, he can have stranle fancies without being mad. The people say I am mad, because I'm willing to wait," he went on. " But lam not. Nothing can make me doubt her." " And did she tell you she would come back ?" I asked. He nodded. " And she never broke her word to me. She could not. She is the soul of truth. The people have a story that she left St. Uny to go away and wed another but that is a lie. I know what she told me, and I believe her. " And what did she tell you ?" He looked at me steadily. "I need not tell you. You know," he replied. Outside the stamps fell on the stones with the same dull monotonous thud, while the water flowed over the wheel. " What day is it ?" he asked Presently. " Friday." "But the date ?" " It is the thirtieth of March." "Yea, that is right. • and then his voice died away in a murmur. "Would you mind going away now?" he said presently. "I don't want to be dis- courteous, but if you knew you would under- stand."

--77 Certainly," I said. "Good-night." "You don't think me rude, do you ?" "Not at all. May I come again to-mor- row night ?" "No, not to-morrow night," he replied quickly. " When then ?"

"Oh, any time after to-morrow. Nothing will matter then."

I puzzled a great deal to know what he meant by this; but I could understand to- thing. I concluded however that he fancied something of importance would happen. In spite of what he had said therefore, I made my way towards the stamps, soon after sun- set.

"Of course I will not let him see me," I reflected, "but I should like to know that all is well with him." -

I therefore went to a sheltered spot with- in sight of the stamp watcher's hut, and sat down on some dried bracken. Why I did this I can hardly tell, but I had a feeling that something would happen. In the near distance the stamps continued to work. At twelve o'clock I knew it would be his duty Ito turn off the water, for the Cornish folk I still keep the Sabbath day very religiously. The night Was very warm, I have known many a July night to be colder; and the moon which was almost at its full made the night almost as bright 26 day. No sound came from the little hut, although I could see the flicker of the light within. How long I sat Ido not know; but suddenly I heard a footstep, and looking I saw the form of a woman approaching the hut. Evident- ly Paul Pentenwin had heard the footsteps. for I saw him come to the door, and look eagerly around. 'Tis she !—'tis she !" I heard him cry. The woman came nearer, and nearer. She was no country woman as could be easily seen by her rich attire. "Paul, Paul," I heard her cry. • 'Betty !" said Paul and then they were locked in each other's arms. As soon as I could I crept silently away. I felt that it would be sacrilege for me to re- main. • •

CRICKET.

YORKSHIRE.

A Great Bowling Feat.

Yorkshire, the nursery of wonderful bowlers; produced yet .another "record" on Saturday, Drake, the old Sheffield United footballer, tak- ing four wickets with consecutive balls for his county versus Derbyshire at Chesterfield. This and Rhodes' modest help to the extent of four wickets for twelve runs enabled Yorkshire to win with great ease..

There had been a lot of rain in the Chester- field district during Thursday night, and when Derbyshire went in a second time, twenty min- utes before luncheon, in a minority of 116, the odds were against their being able to save the innings defeat. At 59 Drake relieved Booth, and five later Slater was clean bowled. At 67 Cadman was leg•before to Rhodes, and the only run scored afterwards was a single with which Morton completed his 50, out of a total of 68. Immediately afterwards Drake created a sensa- tion by getting rid of Baggallay, Curgenven, Hurt and Forester in four successive balls. Horsley was leg-before, and the innings thus came to an ignominious close. It was a left- hander's wicket all over, and Rhodes and Drake certainly made the most of it. It is &wen years since a bowler took four wickets with consecutive balls in county cricket. Albert Trott and Tarrant did this feat for Mid- dlesex in 1907 against Somerset and Gloucester- shire respectively. Trott in the same innings did a second and separate " hat trick," which stands as a record. ' Other cases in which four wickets have been taken with as many balls are: J. Wells, ,for Kent, against Sussex, 1862; George Ulyett, for England, v. New South Wales in Sydney, Aus- tralia, 1872; G. Nash, for Lancashire v. Somer- set, 1882; J. E. Shilton, for Warwickshire v. Lancashire, 1888; Jesse B. Hide, for Sussex' v. the M.C.C., 1890; W. H. Lockwood, for Surrey v. Warwickshire, 1891; Frank Shacklock, for Nottinghamshire v. Somerset, 1893; F. Martin, for the M.C.C. v. Derbyshire, 1895; Arthur Mold, for Lancashire v. Nottinghamshire, 1905; and Mr. Walter Brearley, for Lancashire v. Somerset, 1905. DRAKE EXAMPLE FOLLOWED.

At Edgbaston on Wednesday S. G. Smith, the Northamptonshire captain, performed the best bowling feat of his lifetime, for he finished off the Warwickshire innings by taking four wickets in the same number of balls. He did the "hat trick" at Leicester some years ago, but he had never before taken four wickets with successive deliveries.

WARRINGTON'S FINE WIN. An Easy Victory at Ashton. Thanks to fine use of bat and ball, the War- rington senior team were able to return home from Ashton-under-Lyne on Saturday with a good victory to their credit. Ashton, who were said to have their strongest side out, were dis- posed of at the cost of 105 runs, made up by nearly each man making small totals, instead of through any brilliant hitting. Warrington, on the other hand came in, and after a short time the visiting batsmen had got the mark of the home bowlers. Runs then came along at a steady rate and with the arrival of time and with only six wickets down, Warring- ton had won by 86 runs, their total for the six wickets proving 191. It was singular that T. Wright should have knocked up 86, which was the same as the majority by whicn his side won. It was a happy
